Initial data for Minkowski stability with arbitrary decay
Allen Juntao Fang, Jérémie Szeftel and Arthur Touati

Abstract
We construct and parametrize solutions to the constraint equations of general relativity in a neighborhood of Minkowski spacetime with arbitrary prescribed decay properties at infinity. We thus provide a large class of initial data for the results on stability of Minkowski which include a mass term in the asymptotics. Due to the symmetries of Minkowski, a naive linear perturbation fails. Our construction is based on a simplified conformal method, a reduction to transverse traceless perturbations and a nonlinear fixed point argument where we face linear obstructions coming from the cokernels of both the linearized constraint operator and the Laplace operator. To tackle these obstructions, we introduce a well-chosen truncated black hole around which to perturb. The control of the parameters of the truncated black hole is the most technical part of the proof, since its center of mass and angular momentum could be arbitrarily large.



1
Introduction

In this article, we construct rapidly decaying initial data for the spacelike initial value problem in general relativity.

1.1
The initial value problem in general relativity

At the heart of general relativity are the Einstein vacuum equations, which are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ricci}({\bf g})

\displaystyle=0,

(1.1)

where
{\bf g}
is a Lorentzian metric defined on a 4-dimensional manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{Ricci}({\bf g})
is the Ricci tensor of
{\bf g}
. We refer the reader to
[
CB09
]
for a rich presentation of the mathematical study of the equation (
1.1
) and its equivalent with matter sources.

The geometric equation (
1.1
) in fact corresponds to a Cauchy problem. The Cauchy data, i.e. initial data, are
(\Sigma,g,k)
, where
\Sigma
is a 3-dimensional manifold,
g
is a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
and
k
is a symmetric 2-tensor on
\Sigma
. Solving (
1.1
) with the data
(\Sigma,g,k)
then amounts to constructing a 4-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(M,{\bf g})
with vanishing Ricci tensor and such that
\Sigma
is a spacelike hypersurface in
M
with first and second fundamental forms given by
(g,k)
. This last requirement simply means that
g
is the induced metric on
\Sigma
by
{\bf g}
and that
k=-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\bf T}{\bf g}
, where
{\bf T}
is the future-directed unit normal to
\Sigma
for
{\bf g}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
is the Lie derivative. In her seminal article
[
CB52
]
, Choquet-Bruhat showed that the Cauchy problem described above is locally solvable if and only if the data satisfy the so-called constraint equations

\left\{\begin{aligned} R(g)-|k|^{2}_{g}+(\mathrm{tr}_{g}k)^{2}&=0,\\
\mathrm{div}_{g}k-\mathrm{d}\mathrm{tr}_{g}k&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.2)

on the manifold
\Sigma
, where
R(g)
is the scalar curvature of
g
,
|k|^{2}_{g}=g^{ij}g^{ab}k_{ia}k_{jb}
,
\mathrm{tr}_{g}k=g^{ij}k_{ij}
and
\mathrm{div}_{g}k_{\ell}=g^{ij}D_{i}k_{j\ell}
, where
D
is the covariant derivative associated to
g
. This nonlinear system for
(g,k)
is of elliptic nature and a wealth of literature has been produced on the subject, see the review
[
Car21
]
.

The task of solving (
1.1
) thus factorizes nicely into an elliptic problem for the data and a hyperbolic problem for the evolution. We focus in this paper on the elliptic side, but construct initial data designed to match the requirements of an important question from the hyperbolic side, i.e. the stability of Minkowski spacetime.

1.2
Stability of Minkowski spacetime

The Minkowski spacetime
({\mathbb{R}}^{1+3},{\bf m})
with
{\bf m}
given in Cartesian coordinates by

\displaystyle{\bf m}=-(\mathrm{d}t)^{2}+(\mathrm{d}x^{1})^{2}+(\mathrm{d}x^{2})^{2}+(\mathrm{d}x^{3})^{2},

is the simplest solution to (
1.1
) and corresponds to the framework of special relativity. As a stationary solution of (
1.1
), the question of its nonlinear asymptotic stability is of great importance. More precisely, if one considers asymptotically flat initial data close to the Minkowski data
({\mathbb{R}}^{3},e,0)
, can one show that the resulting spacetime
(M,{\bf g})
is geodesically complete and converges back to Minkowski in a particular gauge?

This question has been answered positively first in the seminal work
[
CK93
]
, and has been revisited several times since. We would like to classify these various results depending on the decay of
(g,k)-(e,0)
at infinity. More precisely, the data are such that
1
1

1

We denote by
\mathbb{N}
the set of nonnegative integers and by
\mathbb{N}^{*}
the set of positive integers.

\displaystyle|g-e|+r|k|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{(1+r)^{q+\delta}},\qquad q\in\mathbb{N},\quad 0<\delta<1,

(1.3)

with obvious extension to derivatives of
g
and
k
. If
q\geq 1
, a mass term decaying like
r^{-1}
needs to be included in (
1.3
); we will be very precise on this issue later in the introduction but for now we simply divide into two categories a (non-exhaustive) list of results on the stability of Minkowski. First, there are the results with
q=0
:

•

[
Bie10
]
deals with
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
,

•

[
LM22
]
deals with
\frac{1}{2}<\delta<1
for self-gravitating massive fields,

•

[
IP22
]
deals with
\delta=1-\delta^{\prime}
(with
\delta^{\prime}>0
small) for the Einstein-Klein-Gordon,

•

[
She23a
]
deals with the full range
0<\delta<1
.

Then come the results with
q\geq 1
:

•

[
CK93
]
deals with
q=1
and
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
,

•

[
LR10
]
,
[
HV20
]
and
[
Hin23
]
deal with
q=1
and the full range
0<\delta<1
.

While the above results concern the global stability of Minkowski, i.e. the geodesic completeness of the maximal globally hyperbolic development of the initial data, we also mention three results, still in the case
q\geq 1
, proving stability of Minkowski restricted to the outside of a far-away outgoing light cone:

•

[
KN03a
]
deals with
q=1
and
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
,

•

[
KN03b
]
deals with
q\geq 3
,
0<\delta<1
and applications to peeling estimates and the smoothness of null infinity,

•

[
She23b
]
deals with
q\geq 1
and
0<\delta<1
.

Since hyperbolic estimates propagate decay in the evolution, stronger spatial decay on the data implies stronger spacetime decay of the perturbations, which in turn provides better control of nonlinear terms. Therefore, for the evolution problem, the more decay in
r
in the initial data, the easier it is to prove stability of Minkowski.

On the other hand, for the elliptic problem, the opposite occurs. Constructing initial data is easier the lower the rate of
r
-decay specified. This is related to the idea that the construction of initial data requires the inversion of elliptic operators in weighted Sobolev spaces. These operators are invertible when
q=0
, and initial data for the articles corresponding to
q=0
can easily be constructed (see for example
[
CBIY00
]
). The goal of this article is to construct and parametrize solutions of the constraint equations near Minkowski spacetime with
q\geq 1
and
0<\delta<1
both arbitrary
2
2

2

The case
\delta=0
cannot be considered here since we rely on Frehdolm properties of the Laplacian which are well-known to fail on weighted Sobolev spaces with integer power decay.
. In particular, this constructs the initial data needed for the stability of Minkowski proved in
[
CK93
,
KN03a
,
KN03b
,
LR10
,
HV20
,
Hin23
,
She23b
]
and it is nontrivial as an elliptic problem.

1.3
Different approaches for the constraint equations

We now focus on the constraint equations (
1.2
). The constraint equations form an underdetermined system of four equations for twelve unknowns, and thus the difficulty is not to find
some
solutions but rather to parametrize the space of
all
solutions. The most successful strategy is the conformal method, first introduced in
[
Lic44
]
. In its most basic form, it considers solutions of the constraint equations of the form

\displaystyle(g,k)=\left(\varphi^{4}\bar{g},\varphi^{-2}\left(\sigma+K_{\bar{g}}W\right)+\frac{\tau}{3}\varphi^{4}\bar{g}\right),

where
\bar{g}
is a Riemannian metric,
\sigma
is a traceless and divergence-free 2-tensor and
\tau
is the mean curvature, and the unknowns are
\varphi
and the vector field
W
(with
K_{\bar{g}}W={\mathcal{L}}_{W}\bar{g}-\frac{2}{3}(\mathrm{div}_{\bar{g}}W)\bar{g}
). The system (
1.2
) is then recast as a semilinear elliptic system for
(\varphi,W)
. This strategy has proved to be very efficient in constructing solutions on compact manifolds with constant mean curvature (CMC) or not (see for instance
[
Ise95
]
and
[
Max09
]
) or on asymptotically flat manifolds (see for instance
[
CBIY00
]
and
[
DIMM14
]
). We also mention the recent drift formulation from
[
Max21
]
aimed at exploring far from CMC regions of the space of solutions.

As mentionned at the end of the previous section, from the point of view of stability of Minkowski or more generally of perturbative regimes in the case
q\geq 1
, the conformal method and its refinements do not produce appropriate initial data, since it always relies on
\Delta_{\bar{g}}
being an isomorphism on fields decaying like
r^{-\delta}
for
0<\delta<1
(thus corresponding to
q=0
in (
1.3
)), a fact demonstrated for asymptotically flat metric
\bar{g}
in the seminal
[
Bar86
]
. If one is interested in stronger and stronger decay, one must take into account the larger and larger cokernel of
\Delta_{\bar{g}}
.

Focusing now on the asymptotically flat case, another successful approach has been the various gluing techniques. Starting with the seminal paper
[
Cor00
]
followed by
[
CD03
]
and
[
CS06
]
, the idea is to glue two solutions of (
1.2
), and thus create an interesting third one, equal to an arbitrary solution when restricted to a compact set and to a member of a family of reference solutions outside of a larger compact set. The gluing
per se
happens in a far away annulus, and the family of reference solutions is often taken as the family of Kerr initial data sets. Because of the nontrivial cokernel of the linearized constraint operator around the Minkowski data (denoted
D\Phi[e,0]
later in this article), this gluing strategy faces linear obstructions with the following consequence: one cannot choose the Kerr initial data set to which one glues. Recently,
[
CR22
]
and
[
MOT23
]
have been able to produce obstruction-free gluing results, i.e. they show how one can choose the Kerr initial data set to which one glues.

From the point of view of perturbative regimes, solutions produced by gluing techniques are too restrictive since they coincide with an exact solution outside of a compact set. Thanks to the finite speed of propagation, the corresponding stability problem is then localized inside an outgoing cone (see for example
[
LR05
]
). Note that in
[
CD03
]
stationnary spacetimes outside of a compact set are constructed via gluing, they are more general than exact black holes solutions but aren’t generic perturbations.

In order to construct nontrivial initial data for the stability of Minkowski problem, i.e. data close to
(e,0)
, arbitrarily decaying and not restricted to a compact set, we employ a simplified version of the conformal method and also face linear obstructions which now come from the intersection
\mathrm{coker}(\Delta)\cap\mathrm{coker}(D\Phi[e,0])
. The fact that this intersection is not trivial forces us to include and control truncated black holes of small masses in the asymptotics, very much as in gluing constructions. While the truncated black hole solutions that we consider have small mass, we will show that they will have potentially large center of mass and angular momentum. Controlling these potentially large parameters for the truncated black hole solutions is a primary complication in the analysis.

1.4
First version of the main result

In this section we present a first version of our main result, see Theorem
3.8
for the precise statement. First, we reformulate the constraint equations (
1.2
) by introducing the reduced second fundamental form

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=k-(\mathrm{tr}_{g}k)g.

Note that this relation is invertible, i.e.
k=\pi-\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}_{g}\pi)g
. The constraint equations for the unknown
(g,\pi)
read

\left\{\begin{aligned} R(g)+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}_{g}\pi)^{2}-|\pi|^{2}_{g}&=0,\\
\mathrm{div}_{g}\pi&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.4)

For further use, we introduce the notations

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(g,\pi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=R(g)+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}_{g}\pi)^{2}-|\pi|^{2}_{g},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}(g,\pi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathrm{div}_{g}\pi,

\displaystyle\Phi(g,\pi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=({\mathcal{H}}(g,\pi),{\mathcal{M}}(g,\pi)),

so that (
1.4
) rewrites
\Phi(g,\pi)=0
. For
X
a vector field on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and
g
a Riemannian metric we define

\displaystyle L_{g}X\vcentcolon={\mathcal{L}}_{X}g-(\mathrm{div}_{g}X)g.

The symmetric 2-tensors defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
which are traceless and divergence free with respect to the Euclidean metric
e
play a major role in this article, and such a tensor is said to be TT (for traceless and transverse). When needed, we will also denote by
TT
the set of TT tensors.

Theorem 1.1

(Main result, version 1)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
0<\delta<1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
be TT tensors with

\displaystyle\sup_{k=0,1}\left(r^{k}|\nabla^{k}\widecheck{g}|+r^{k+1}|\nabla^{k}\widecheck{\pi}|\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{(1+r)^{q+\delta}},

(1.5)

and set
\eta^{2}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. If
\eta>0
and if
\varepsilon
is small enough, then there exists a solution of (
1.4
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
which for large
r
is of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=g_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{g}+4\widecheck{u}e+O\left(\varepsilon r^{-q-\delta-1}\right),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{\pi}+L_{e}\widecheck{X}+O\left(\varepsilon r^{-q-\delta-2}\right).

Here,
(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})
is a Kerr initial data set of mass
m
, center of mass
y
and angular momentum
a
such that

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},\qquad|y|+|a|\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{2}{2q+2\delta-1}},

(1.7)

and the scalar function
\widecheck{u}
and the vector field
\widecheck{X}
satisfy

\displaystyle|\widecheck{u}|+\left|\widecheck{X}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{(1+r)^{q+\delta}}.

Some comments are in order.

•

In Theorem
1.1
we actually construct a map from the ”seed” decaying TT tensors
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
to the space of solutions to (
1.4
), i.e. both the Kerr initial data set
(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})
and the correctors
\left(\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)
are constructed out of
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
. In Proposition
3.11
, we will show that this map is injective so that formally we construct a graph of solutions over the set of decaying TT tensors. This cannot be seen on the expansion (
1.11
) and requires the exact statement of Theorem
3.8
. The assumption that our seed tensors
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
are TT might seem restrictive but we will actually show that thanks to the diffeomorphism invariance it is generic in a neighborhood of Minkowski, see Section
3.2
.

•

As announced at the end of the previous section, the center of mass
y
and the angular momentum
a
of the constructed Kerr initial data set
(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})
are large. Indeed, the decay assumption (
1.5
) implies that
\eta\lesssim\varepsilon
, so that
\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{2}{2q+2\delta-1}}
appearing in (
1.8
) is potentially large. The reason for this will be explained in the next section where we will in particular explain how
(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})
is constructed. We can already say that the largeness of
y
and
a
is the main technical difficulty in our proof, where we will often crucially need to extract the small constant
\eta
instead of the small (but larger) constant
\varepsilon
. Note that the largeness of
\frac{|a|}{m}
does not contradict the Penrose inequality since there are no apparent horizons in the resulting manifold constructed by Theorem
1.1
(we will introduce cutoffs such that we don’t see the black hole’s horizon).

•

The solutions constructed in Theorem
1.1
can be glued to interior solutions of the constraint equations using the obstruction-free gluing results of
[
CR22
]
and
[
MOT23
]
, thus providing a large number of solutions that asymptote to trivial initial data at arbitrary rate.

•

While in this paper we focus on the case
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the case
q=0
is well-known and significantly simpler thanks to the good inversion properties of the Laplacian in this range. See for instance
[
Hua10
]
, where the author constructs perturbations around any asymptotically flat solution of the constraint equations for
q=0
and
\delta\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)
.

Of independent interest is the following corollary (see Corollary
3.9
for a precise statement) where solutions to
R(g)=0
are constructed in a neighborhood of Minkowski.

Corollary 1.2

(Time-symmetric case, version 1)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
0<\delta<1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\widecheck{g}
be a TT tensor with

\displaystyle\sup_{k=0,1}r^{k}|\nabla^{k}\widecheck{g}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{(1+r)^{q+\delta}},

and set
\eta^{2}\vcentcolon=\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. If
\eta>0
and if
\varepsilon
is small enough, then there exists a solution of
R(g)=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
which for large
r
is of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=g_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{g}+4\widecheck{u}e+O\left(\varepsilon r^{-q-\delta-1}\right).

Here,
g_{\vec{p}}
is a Schwarzschild initial data set of mass
m
and center of mass
y
such that

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},\qquad|y|\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{2}{2q+2\delta-1}},

(1.8)

and the scalar function
\widecheck{u}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\widecheck{u}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{(1+r)^{q+\delta}}.

1.5
Sketch of proof

In this section, we intend to present the main features of our proof. We first consider so-called ”seed” initial data in the form of
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})\in S^{2}T^{*}\Sigma\times S^{2}T^{*}\Sigma
a pair of symmetric 2-tensors over the initial slice
\Sigma
, and search for solutions of (
1.4
) of the form

\displaystyle(g,\pi)=\left(u^{4}(e+\widecheck{g}),\widecheck{\pi}+L_{e}X\right).

(1.9)

This is a simplification of the ansatz proposed in
[
CS06
]
, where
\pi
would be given by
\pi=u^{2}(\widecheck{\pi}+L_{e+\widecheck{g}}X)
. The constraint equations (
1.4
) then reads

\displaystyle\Delta\left(u-1,-X\right)=\Phi(e+\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})+\text{lower order terms},

(1.10)

where
\Delta
is the standard Euclidean Laplacian operator. In this discussion we completely neglect the lower order terms. If
\widecheck{g}
and
\widecheck{\pi}
satisfy (
1.5
) then thanks to standard properties of
\Delta
in weighted Sobolev spaces we can invert (
1.10
) and obtain

\displaystyle\left(u-1,-X\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=-(j-1)}^{j-1}\left\langle\Phi(e+\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right\rangle{\mathcal{V}}_{j,\ell}+\left(\tilde{u},-\tilde{X}\right),

(1.11)

where
{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}
denotes roughly a basis of homogeneous polynomials
3
3

3

These are in fact harmonic polynomials and vectorial harmonic polynomials, see Definition
2.4
.
of degree
j-1
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{j,\ell}
decays like
r^{-j}
,
\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{X}
are small remainders decaying like
r^{-q-\delta}
and
\langle f,h\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}fh
. We actually have

\displaystyle\mathrm{coker}\left(\Delta\right)=\mathrm{Span}\left({\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell},1\leq j\leq q,-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1\right),

(1.12)

if
\Delta
denotes the operator
4
4

4

Here,
H^{2}_{-q-\delta}
and
L^{2}_{-q-\delta-2}
denote weighted Sobolev spaces, see Definition
2.2
.

\Delta:H^{2}_{-q-\delta}\longrightarrow L^{2}_{-q-\delta-2}
(acting on couples formed by a scalar function and a vector field expressed in Cartesian coordinates). See Section
2.2
for complete details on the inversion of an equation like (
1.10
). In order to prove our theorem, we need to cancel the scalar products appearing in (
1.11
). If we expand the constraint operator
\Phi
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\Phi(e+\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),D\Phi[e,0]^{*}\left({\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right)\right\rangle+\text{nonlinear terms}.

(1.13)

where
D\Phi[e,0]
is the first variation of the constraint operator at
(e,0)
and
D\Phi[e,0]^{*}
denotes the
L^{2}
-formal adjoint operator. A naive way to generate solutions to the constraint equations that decay like
r^{-q-\delta}
near infinity would then be to find compact perturbations
(\breve{g},\breve{\pi})
such that

\displaystyle\left\langle(\breve{g},\breve{\pi}),D\Phi[e,0]^{*}(\mathcal{W}_{j,\ell})\right\rangle=-\left\langle(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),D\Phi[e,0]^{*}(\mathcal{W}_{j,\ell})\right\rangle

to linearly cancel out the scalar product on the RHS of (
1.13
) and linearly absorb

\left\langle(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),D\Phi[e,0]^{*}\left({\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right)\right\rangle.

This however requires
D\Phi[e,0]^{*}\left({\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right)
to be non-zero. Thus, according to (
1.12
), we need to understand the intersection

\displaystyle\mathrm{coker}\left(\Delta\right)\cap\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right).

(1.14)

The famous result of
[
Mon75
]
tells us that elements of
\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[g,\pi]\right)
(usually called KIDS in the literature, for Killing Initial Data Sets) are identified with the projections on the initial hypersurface under consideration of the Killing fields of the spacetime emanating from the initial data set
(g,\pi)
. The Killing fields of Minkowski are the four translations
\partial_{\mu}
, the three rotations
x_{i}\partial_{j}-x_{j}\partial_{i}
and the three boosts
t\partial_{i}+x_{i}\partial_{t}
. It is a straightforward exercise to check that the associated Killing initial data sets of the Killing fields of Minkowski in fact all belong to
\mathrm{coker}\left(\Delta\right)
so that

\mathrm{coker}\left(\Delta\right)\cap\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right)=\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right)

forms a 10-dimensional space of linear obstructions.

Remark 1.3
.

In this regard, the Minkowski spacetime is the hardest case to treat. First, it is maximally symmetric, i.e. the space of Killing vector fields has the largest dimension possible (10 for a 3+1 spacetime) and, thanks to
[
Mon75
]
, so does
\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[g,\pi]\right)
. Second, the intersection (
1.14
) is maximal since
\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right)\subset\mathrm{coker}\left(\Delta\right)
.

We modify the ansatz (
1.9
) in order to deal with the scalar products in (
1.11
): we introduce compactly supported perturbations
\breve{g}
and
\breve{\pi}
, and a truncated black hole initial data set
(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}})
and consider the now complete ansatz

\displaystyle(g,\pi)=\left(u^{4}(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}+\breve{g}),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{\pi}+\breve{\pi}+L_{e}X\right).

(1.15)

The family of
(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}})
are constructed in Section
2.4
following
[
CD03
]
. They are parametrized by the black hole parameter
\vec{p}=(m,y,a,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
where
m
is the mass of the black hole,
y
is the center of mass of the black hole,
a
is the angular momentum of the black hole and
v
corresponds to a Lorentz boost. The cutoff
\chi_{\vec{p}}
is equal to 0 if
r\leq\lambda
and to 1 if
r\geq 2\lambda
where
\lambda=\max(2|y|,2|a|,3)
. They precisely form a 10-dimensional family of asymptotics. The goal will now be to choose the correct black hole parameter
\vec{p}
and compact perturbation
(\breve{g},\breve{\pi})
so that
u
and
X
in (
1.15
) decay like
r^{-q-\delta}
.

1.5.1
The system for the black hole parameter

The 10-dimensional family of
(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}})
are added to cancel the scalar products
\left\langle\Phi(e+\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right\rangle
in the case where
{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\in\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right)
. Recall that this is precisely the case where the leading term in (
1.13
) are the quadratic terms. Thus, the black hole needs to solve

\displaystyle\left\langle D\Phi[e,0](\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}),{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\frac{1}{2}D^{2}\Phi[e,0]((\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})),{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right\rangle+\mathrm{l.o.t},

(1.16)

where
D^{2}\Phi[e,0]
is the second variation of the constraint operator. As we will show using the well-known asymptotics charges (see for example
[
CD03
]
), the expressions on the LHS of (
1.16
) actually precisely recover the black hole parameters (see Proposition
2.19
).

Lower bound on the mass.

If
{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}
in (
1.16
) corresponds to the time translation, we get the equation for the mass

\displaystyle 16\pi\gamma m

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathrm{div}\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\mathrm{tr}\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathrm{tr}\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathrm{l.o.t},

(1.17)

where
\gamma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-|v|^{2}}}
is the Lorentz factor. Note that
m
is not only the mass of the black hole
(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})
but also the mass of our solution (
1.15
). The positive mass theorem, proved in a neighborhood of Minkowski in
[
CBM76
]
(where only the time-symmetric case is studied) and then in full generality in
[
SY79
]
and
[
Wit81
]
, implies that if
m=0
then
(g,\pi)
is isometric to
(e,0)
. Therefore, to ensure a good parametrization of a neighborhood of
(e,0)
, we need coercivity of the quadratic form appearing in (
1.17
). This is achieved by assuming that
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
are TT tensors (in
[
CBM76
]
harmonic coordinates are used, see also
[
CBFM79
]
), and we show in Section
3.2
that this is generic in a neighborhood of Minkowski up to a choice of diffeomorphism, i.e. up to a choice of a different initial hypersurface. Therefore, (
1.17
) becomes

\displaystyle 16\pi\gamma m

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathrm{l.o.t}.

(1.18)

In particular the mass cannot be zero if
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
are not trivial.

Coupling with the boost.

Looking at (
1.18
), we see that a lower bound on the mass is equivalent to an upper bound on
\gamma
. From (
1.16
) in the case where
{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}
corresponds to space translations, we can get an equation for
v
which, plugged into (
1.18
), finally implies

\displaystyle 16\pi m

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\mathrm{l.o.t},

(1.19)

where the functional
J
is defined by

\displaystyle J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1,2,3}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\widecheck{\pi}^{ij}\partial_{k}\widecheck{g}_{ij}\right)^{2}}}{\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

In Section
3.3
we study the functional
J
and show that it is not uniformly far from 1 on the set of TT tensors. Therefore our construction might degenerate if
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
are such that
J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
goes close to 1, and we need to perform our construction over the set of TT tensors
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
such that
J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})\leq 1-{\alpha}
for some
{\alpha}
arbitrary in
(0,1]
(note that this is a cone in the space of TT tensors). Since
{\alpha}
is a universal constant, (
1.19
) finally implies the lower bound
m\gtrsim\eta^{2}
(where
\eta
is defined in Theorem
1.1
).

Large center of mass and angular momentum.

From (
1.16
) when
{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}
corresponds to the three rotations and the three boosts and from the lower bound on the mass, we then obtain the following bound for the center of mass and angular momentum

\displaystyle|y|+|a|\lesssim\eta^{-2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\left(|\nabla\widecheck{g}|^{2}+|\widecheck{\pi}|^{2}\right)r+\mathrm{l.o.t}.

(1.20)

Because of the
r
weight in (
1.20
) we need to use the decay assumption (
1.5
), which means that we cannot fully compensate the
\eta^{-2}
coming from the mass. The best we can do is to interpolate sharply between weighted and non-weighted norms and get

\displaystyle|y|+|a|\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{2}{2q+2\delta-1}}.

(1.21)

The largeness of
y
and
a
, coming from the two different small constants
\eta
and
\varepsilon
, is at the origin of most of the difficulties we face. Getting
\eta
instead of
\varepsilon
in nonlinear estimates will often be crucial, for instance for the contraction estimates in the fixed point arguments. In particular, the largeness of
y
and
a
implies that the cutoffs
\chi_{\vec{p}}
are localized in far regions and we need to be careful when estimating weighted norms (the power in (
1.21
) will be crucial).

1.5.2
The compact perturbations

The tensors
(\breve{g},\breve{\pi})
are added to cancel the scalar products
\left\langle\Phi(e+\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi}),{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\right\rangle
in the case where
{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\notin\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right)
. Therefore, at leading order they need to solve a finite dimensional and linear system of the form

\left\langle(\breve{g},\breve{\pi}),D\Phi[e,0]^{*}({\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell})\right\rangle={\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\ell},

(1.22)

for some numbers
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\ell}
depending on
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
and for every
{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\notin\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right)
. We solve for
(\breve{g},\breve{\pi})\in{\mathcal{C}}_{q}
with
{\mathcal{C}}_{q}
a finite dimensional space of compactly supported 2-tensors. Since the family

\displaystyle\left(D\Phi[e,0]^{*}({\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell})\right)_{{\mathcal{W}}_{j,\ell}\in\mathrm{coker}\left(\Delta\right)\setminus\mathrm{coker}\left(D\Phi[e,0]\right)}

is linearly independent, a definition of
{\mathcal{C}}_{q}
ensuring the invertibility of (
1.22
) is not difficult. However, as first explained in the remarks following Theorem
1.1
, we want a uniqueness statement i.e. that the map
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})\longmapsto(g,\pi)
defined by (
1.15
) is injective. Therefore, we need

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{q}\cap\left(\left(TT+\mathrm{Span}(e)\right)\times\left(TT+\mathrm{ran}(L_{e})\right)\right)=\{0\}

(1.23)

where
TT
denotes the space of TT tensors and
\mathrm{ran}(L_{e})=\left\{L_{e}X\middle|X\in T\Sigma\right\}
. The construction of
{\mathcal{C}}_{q}
satisfying (
1.23
) is performed in Section
2.3
.

1.6
Outline of the article

We conclude this introduction by giving an outline of the rest of the article.

•

In Section
2
, we introduce the different tools and results necessary to our construction. This includes standard notations, elliptic theory on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, properties of the constraint operators and the family of Kerr initial data sets.

•

In Section
3
, we introduce our simplified conformal formulation of the constraint equations, reduce the space of seed tensors and then state our results.

•

In Section
4
we prove our main result, Theorem
3.8
.

The article is concluded by two appendices: Appendix
A
contains all the proofs of Section
2
and Appendix
B
contains all the proofs of Section
3
.

Acknowledgments

The first author acknowledges support from NSF award DMS-2303241 and support through Germany’s Excellence Strategy EXC 2044 390685587, Mathematics Münster: Dynamics–Geometry–Structure. The second author is supported by the ERC grant ERC-2023 AdG 101141855 BlaHSt.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notations

We will work on the manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with Cartesian coordinates
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
and
r=|x|
with

\displaystyle|x|=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}+(x^{3})^{2}}.

The standard Euclidean metric will be denoted by
e
, i.e.

\displaystyle e=(\mathrm{d}x^{1})^{2}+(\mathrm{d}x^{2})^{2}+(\mathrm{d}x^{3})^{2}.

Latin indexes will always refer to Cartesian coordinates and will be raised with respect to
e
. Moreover, the Einstein summation convention will be used for couples of repeted indexes with one up and one down.

If
f
is a scalar function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
we define its gradient by
\nabla f=(\partial_{1}f,\partial_{2}f,\partial_{3}f)
and its Hessian matrix by
(\mathrm{Hess}f)_{ij}=\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f
. If
X
is a vector field on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
its components in Cartesian coordinates are denoted
X_{i}
and if
T
is a symmetric 2-tensor on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
its components in Cartesian coordinates are denoted
T_{ij}
. Moreover we use the notations

\displaystyle|\nabla X|^{2}

\displaystyle=\partial^{i}X^{j}\partial_{i}X_{j},

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}T

\displaystyle=\delta^{ij}T_{ij},

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}X

\displaystyle=\partial^{i}X_{i},

\displaystyle|\nabla T|^{2}

\displaystyle=\partial^{k}T^{ij}\partial_{k}T_{ij},

\displaystyle\nabla\otimes X_{ij}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}X_{j}+\partial_{j}X_{i},

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}T_{i}

\displaystyle=\partial^{j}T_{ji}.

We recall the following standard definition of a TT tensor.

Definition 2.1
.

A symmetric 2-tensor
S
is called TT when
\mathrm{tr}S=0
and
\mathrm{div}S=0
.

We employ the following standard notations for inequalities:
A\lesssim B
means that
A\leq CB
for
C
a universal constant;
C(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
will denote any polynomial expression (without constant coefficient) of the real numbers
c_{i}>0
;
A\lesssim_{c_{1},\dots,c_{n}}B
means that
A\leq C(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})B
.

Finally, if
E
is a normed vector space, then
B_{E}(x,r)
denotes the open ball of center
x\in E
and radius
r\geq 0
.

2.2
Weighted elliptic theory on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}

We define the usual weighted Sobolev and Hölder spaces on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Note that if not otherwise specified, every function spaces (such as the usual Lebesgue or Sobolev spaces) will be defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(i)

We define the weighted Sobolev spaces
H^{k}_{\delta}
as the completion of the space of smooth and compactly supported functions for the norm

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k}_{\delta}}=\sum_{|{\alpha}|\leq k}\left\|(1+r)^{-\delta-\frac{3}{2}+|{\alpha}|}\nabla^{\alpha}u\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We use the standard notation
L^{2}_{\delta}=H^{0}_{\delta}
.

(ii)

We define the space
C^{k}_{\delta}
as the completion of the space of smooth and compactly supported functions for the norm

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{C^{k}_{\delta}}=\sum_{|{\alpha}|\leq k}\left\|(1+r)^{-\delta+|{\alpha}|}\nabla^{\alpha}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}.

These definition are easily extended to tensors of all kinds by summing over all components in Cartesian coordinates. The spaces
H^{k}_{\delta}
were first introduced in
[
Can75
]
and their properties studied in
[
CBC81
]
(see also the first appendix of
[
CB09
]
). Note that we use Bartnik’s convention from
[
Bar86
]
in order to ease the reading of the decay rates, as shown by next lemma’s first part.

Lemma 2.3
.

We have the following properties:

(i)

If
k>m+\frac{3}{2}
, then we have the continuous embedding
H^{k}_{\delta}\subset C^{m}_{\delta}
.

(ii)

If
k\leq\min(k_{1},k_{2})
,
k<k_{1}+k_{2}-\frac{3}{2}
and
\delta>\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}
we have the continuous embedding

\displaystyle H^{k_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}\times H^{k_{2}}_{\delta_{2}}\subset H^{k}_{\delta}.

Proof.

See
[
CB09
]
.
∎

The following definition contains all the various functions and vector fields required to invert the Laplace operator
\Delta\vcentcolon=\partial^{i}\partial_{i}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

Definition 2.4
.

We introduce the following notations:

(i)

We consider the real spherical harmonics
Y_{j,\ell}
for
j\geq 0
and
-j\leq\ell\leq j
with the following normalization

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}Y_{j,\ell}Y_{j^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=4\pi\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}.

(2.1)

(ii)

For
j\geq 1
and
-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1
, we define two families of functions

\displaystyle w_{j,\ell}

\displaystyle=r^{j-1}Y_{j-1,\ell},

\displaystyle v_{j,\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{\ell+1}Y_{j-1,-\ell}}{4\pi(2j-1)r^{j}}.

(iii)

For
j\geq 1
,
-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1
and
k=1,2,3
we define the vector fields

\displaystyle W_{j,\ell,k}

\displaystyle=w_{j,\ell}\partial_{k},

\displaystyle V_{j,\ell,k}

\displaystyle=v_{j,\ell}\partial_{k}.

For
a,b=1,2,3
, we also define

\displaystyle\Omega_{ab}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=x^{a}\partial_{b}\pm x^{b}\partial_{a}.

(iv)

We also fix a smooth cutoff function
\chi:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\longrightarrow[0,1]
such that
\chi(r)=0
for
r\leq 1
and
\chi(r)=1
for
r\geq 2
.

The following theorem from
[
McO79
]
is our main tool to invert the flat Laplacian in weighted Sobolev spaces on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
-p-1<\rho<-p
. Then
\Delta:H^{2}_{\rho}\longrightarrow L^{2}_{\rho-2}
is an injection with closed range equal to

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\rho-2}\cap\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{p-1}\mathfrak{H}^{i}\right)^{\perp_{L^{2}}},

where
\mathfrak{H}^{i}
is the set of harmonic polynomials defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
which are homogeneous of degree
i
. Moreover we have
\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\left\|\Delta u\right\|_{L^{2}_{\rho-2}}
.

Remark 2.6
.

Also from
[
McO79
]
we have: if
-1<\rho<0
, then
\Delta:H^{2}_{\rho}\longrightarrow L^{2}_{\rho-2}
is an isomorphism and moreover
\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}_{\rho}}\lesssim\left\|\Delta u\right\|_{L^{2}_{\rho-2}}
.

We deduce the following proposition, its proof being postponed to Appendix
A.1
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
0<\delta<1
. Then we have the following:

(i)

If
f
is a scalar function satisfying
f\in L^{2}_{-q-\delta-2}
, then there exists a unique
\tilde{u}\in H^{2}_{-q-\delta}
such that
u
defined by

\displaystyle u=\chi(r)\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=-(j-1)}^{j-1}\langle f,w_{j,\ell}\rangle v_{j,\ell}+\tilde{u}

is a solution to
\Delta u=f
. Moreover, we have
\left\|\tilde{u}\right\|_{H^{2}_{-q-\delta}}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{-q-\delta-2}}
.

(ii)

If
Y
is a vector field satisfying
Y\in L^{2}_{-q-\delta-2}
, then there exists a unique vector field
\tilde{X}\in H^{2}_{-q-\delta}
such that
X
defined by

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\chi(r)\sum_{k=1,2,3}\langle Y,W_{1,0,k}\rangle V_{1,0,k}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\chi(r)\Big(\langle Y,\Omega^{-}_{12}\rangle\left(-V_{2,-1,1}+V_{2,1,2}\right)+\langle Y,\Omega^{-}_{13}\rangle\left(-V_{2,0,1}+V_{2,1,3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 56.9055pt+\langle Y,\Omega^{-}_{23}\rangle\left(-V_{2,0,2}+V_{2,-1,3}\right)+\langle Y,\Omega^{+}_{23}\rangle\left(V_{2,0,2}+V_{2,-1,3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 56.9055pt+\langle Y,\Omega^{+}_{12}\rangle\left(V_{2,-1,1}+V_{2,1,2}\right)+\langle Y,\Omega^{+}_{13}\rangle\left(V_{2,0,1}+V_{2,1,3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 56.9055pt+\langle Y,\Omega^{+}_{11}\rangle V_{2,1,1}+\langle Y,\Omega^{+}_{22}\rangle V_{2,-1,2}+\langle Y,\Omega^{+}_{33}\rangle V_{2,0,3}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\chi(r)\sum_{j=3}^{q}\sum_{\ell=-(j-1)}^{j-1}\sum_{k=1,2,3}\langle Y,W_{j,\ell,k}\rangle V_{j,\ell,k}+\tilde{X}

is a solution to
\Delta X=Y
. Moreover, we have
\left\|\tilde{X}\right\|_{H^{2}_{-q-\delta}}\lesssim\left\|Y\right\|_{L^{2}_{-q-\delta-2}}
.

Remark 2.8
.

In the previous proposition, we performed a change of basis for the space of vector fields with components given by harmonic polynomials homogeneous of degree 1. Instead of the standard basis
(x^{i}\partial_{j})_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}
, we prefered the basis

\displaystyle\left(\Omega^{+}_{11},\Omega^{+}_{22},\Omega^{+}_{33},\Omega^{+}_{12},\Omega^{+}_{13},\Omega^{+}_{23},\Omega^{-}_{12},\Omega^{-}_{13},\Omega^{-}_{23}\right).

This is motivated by the properties of the linearized momentum constraint operator, see Proposition
2.13
.

Remark 2.9
.

We can improve Proposition
2.7
: if the scalar function
f
or the vector field
Y
are in
H^{k}_{-q-\delta-2}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\tilde{u}
or
\tilde{X}
are actually in
H^{k+2}_{-q-\delta}
with the obvious bounds. This also applies for the isomorphism case mentioned in Remark
2.6
. This will only be used (without mention) in Appendix
B.1
and
B.2
.

2.3
The constraint operator

If
\bar{g}
is a Riemannian metric and
\bar{\pi}
a symmetric two-tensor, we denote by
D{\mathcal{H}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
and
D^{2}{\mathcal{H}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
(resp.
D{\mathcal{M}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
and
D^{2}{\mathcal{M}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
) the first and second variation of the operator
{\mathcal{H}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{M}}
) at
(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})
, and recall that
\Phi(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})=\left({\mathcal{H}}(\bar{g},\bar{\pi}),{\mathcal{M}}(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})\right)
. Note that in this article, and in particular in Lemma
2.10
below, we do not distinguish between coefficients of
\bar{g}
and coefficients of its inverse
\bar{g}^{-1}
and thus simply write
\bar{g}
in the schematic notation for error terms.

Lemma 2.10
.

We have

\displaystyle\Phi(\bar{g}+h,\bar{\pi}+\pi)

\displaystyle=\Phi(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})+D\Phi[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}](h,\pi)+\frac{1}{2}D^{2}\Phi[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]((h,\pi),(h,\pi))+\mathrm{Err}\Phi[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}](h,\pi),

(2.2)

where schematically

\displaystyle D{\mathcal{H}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}](h,\pi)

\displaystyle=\bar{g}\nabla^{2}h+h\nabla^{2}\bar{g}+\bar{g}^{2}\nabla\bar{g}\nabla h+\bar{g}h(\nabla\bar{g})^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\bar{\pi}\pi+\bar{g}\bar{\pi}^{2}h,

(2.3)

\displaystyle D{\mathcal{M}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}](h,\pi)

\displaystyle=\bar{g}\nabla\pi+h\nabla\bar{\pi}+\bar{g}^{2}\nabla\bar{g}\pi+\bar{g}^{2}\bar{\pi}\nabla h+\bar{g}\bar{\pi}h\nabla\bar{g},

(2.4)

\displaystyle D^{2}{\mathcal{H}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]((h,\pi),(h,\pi))

\displaystyle=h\nabla^{2}h+\bar{g}h\nabla\bar{g}\nabla h+(\bar{g}\nabla h)^{2}+(h\nabla\bar{g})^{2}

(2.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\bar{g}\bar{\pi}h\pi+(\bar{g}\pi)^{2}+(\bar{\pi}h)^{2},

(2.6)

\displaystyle D^{2}{\mathcal{M}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]((h,\pi),(h,\pi))

\displaystyle=\bar{g}^{2}h\nabla\pi+\bar{g}h\pi\nabla\bar{g}+\bar{g}\bar{\pi}h\nabla h+h^{2}\bar{\pi}\nabla\bar{g},

(2.7)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}{\mathcal{H}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}](h,\pi)

\displaystyle=\bar{g}h(\nabla h)^{2}+h^{2}\nabla\bar{g}\nabla h+(h\nabla h)^{2}+\bar{\pi}h^{2}\pi+(h\pi)^{2},

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}{\mathcal{M}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}](h,\pi)

\displaystyle=\bar{g}h\pi\nabla h+h^{2}\pi\nabla\bar{g}+h^{2}\bar{\pi}\nabla h+h^{2}\pi\nabla h.

(2.9)

Proof.

Straightforward computations from the schematic expressions

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})

\displaystyle=\bar{g}\nabla^{2}\bar{g}+(\bar{g}\nabla\bar{g})^{2}+(\bar{g}\bar{\pi})^{2},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})

\displaystyle=\bar{g}\left(\nabla\bar{\pi}+\bar{g}\bar{\pi}\nabla\bar{g}\right).

See also
[
FMM80
]
for the exact expressions of
D\Phi[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
and
D^{2}\Phi[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
.
∎

Of particular importance is the case of the Minkowski initial data set
(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})=(e,0)
, for which we give the exact expressions of both the first and second variations of the constraint operator.

Lemma 2.11
.

We have
5
5

5

Note that
\pi
doesn’t appear in
D{\mathcal{H}}[e,0](h,\pi)
and
h
doesn’t appear in
D{\mathcal{M}}[e,0](h,\pi)
so we simply denote them by
D{\mathcal{H}}[e,0](h)
and
D{\mathcal{M}}[e,0](\pi)
throughout the paper.

\displaystyle D{\mathcal{H}}[e,0](h)

\displaystyle=-\Delta\mathrm{tr}h+\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}D^{2}{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]((h,\pi),(h,\pi))

\displaystyle=-2h^{ij}\partial_{i}\mathrm{div}h_{j}+h^{ij}\Delta h_{ij}+h^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\mathrm{tr}h-|\mathrm{div}h|^{2}

(2.11)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{div}h\cdot\nabla\mathrm{tr}h-\frac{1}{4}|\nabla\mathrm{tr}h|^{2}+\frac{3}{4}|\nabla h|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{b}h^{jk}\partial_{j}h_{kb}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}\pi)^{2}-|\pi|^{2},

and

\displaystyle D{\mathcal{M}}[e,0](\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}\pi,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}D^{2}{\mathcal{M}}[e,0]((h,\pi),(h,\pi))_{i}

\displaystyle=-h^{k\ell}\partial_{k}\pi_{\ell i}-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{k\ell}\partial_{i}h_{k\ell}-\pi_{i}^{k}\left(\mathrm{div}h_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{k}\mathrm{tr}h\right).

(2.13)

Proof.

See
[
FMM80
]
.
∎

When solving the constraint equations around a given solution
(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})
, the linear obstructions come from elements of the cokernel of the linearized operators
D{\mathcal{H}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
and
D{\mathcal{M}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
f
be a scalar function and
X
a vector field. We say that
(f,X)
is a KIDS for
(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})
if
D{\mathcal{H}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]^{*}(f,X)=0
and
D{\mathcal{M}}[\bar{g},\bar{\pi}]^{*}(f,X)=0
.

In the case of the Euclidean metric, we have

\displaystyle D{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]^{*}(f)

\displaystyle=-(\Delta f)e+\mathrm{Hess}f,

\displaystyle D{\mathcal{M}}[e,0]^{*}(X)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\otimes X,

for
f
a scalar function and
X
a vector field. The following proposition, whose proof is postponed to Appendix
A.2
, identifies in particular the KIDS of
(e,0)
.

Proposition 2.13
.

The operator
D{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]^{*}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

The kernel of
D{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]^{*}
is generated by
w_{j,\ell}
for
j=1,2
and
-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1
.

(ii)

For
q\geq 3
, there exists a family of symmetric 2-tensors
(h_{j,\ell})_{3\leq j\leq q,-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1}
such that:

–

the tensors
h_{j,\ell}
are traceless, smooth and compactly supported in
\{1\leq r\leq 10\}
,

–

the matrix

\displaystyle\left(\left\langle h_{j,\ell},D{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]^{*}(w_{j^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})\right\rangle\right)_{3\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq q,-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1,-(j^{\prime}-1)\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq j^{\prime}-1}

is invertible,

–

the family of 1-form
\left(\mathrm{div}h_{j,\ell}\right)_{3\leq j\leq q,-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1}
is linearly independent.

The operator
D{\mathcal{M}}[e,0]^{*}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

The kernel of
D{\mathcal{M}}[e,0]^{*}
is generated by
\left\{W_{1,0,1},W_{1,0,2},W_{1,0,3},\Omega^{-}_{12},\Omega^{-}_{13},\Omega^{-}_{23}\right\}
.

(ii)

For
q\geq 2
, there exists a family of symmetric 2-tensors
(\varpi_{Z})_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{q}}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{2}\vcentcolon=\left\{\Omega^{+}_{11},\Omega^{+}_{22},\Omega^{+}_{33},\Omega^{+}_{12},\Omega^{+}_{13},\Omega^{+}_{23}\right\},

and if
q\geq 3

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{q}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{Z}_{2}\cup\left\{W_{j,\ell,k}\,\middle|\,3\leq j\leq q,-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1,k=1,2,3\right\},

such that:

–

the tensors
\varpi_{Z}
are smooth and compactly supported in
\{1\leq r\leq 10\}
,

–

the matrix

\displaystyle\left(\left\langle\varpi_{Z},D{\mathcal{M}}[e,0]^{*}(Z^{\prime})\right\rangle\right)_{(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Z}_{q}^{2}}

is invertible,

–

the family of functions
\left((\Delta\mathrm{tr}+\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div})\varpi_{Z}\right)_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{q}}
is linearly independent.

Definition 2.14
.

For
q\geq 3
, we define the space

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{q}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Span}\Big(h_{j,\ell},3\leq j\leq q,-(j-1)\leq\ell\leq j-1\Big).

For
q\geq 2
, we define the space

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{q}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Span}\left(\varpi_{Z},Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{q}\right).

Note that
\dim{\mathcal{A}}_{q}=q^{2}-4
and
\dim{\mathcal{B}}_{q}=3q^{2}-6
.

Since elements of
{\mathcal{A}}_{q}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{q}
are smooth and supported in
\{1\leq r\leq 10\}
, we will simply use the
W^{2,\infty}
and
W^{1,\infty}
topology on these spaces. This corresponds to the maximal degree of differentiation in the constraint equations for the metric and the reduced second fundamental form.

Remark 2.15
.

If one defines
{\mathcal{C}}_{q}={\mathcal{A}}_{q}\times{\mathcal{B}}_{q}
, we note that
{\mathcal{C}}_{q}
does indeed satisfy (
1.23
). This will be crucially used in the proof of Proposition
3.11
.

2.4
Kerr initial data sets

In this section, we construct and study the family of Kerr initial data sets, following Appendix F of
[
CD03
]
(see also
[
ACR23
]
).

2.4.1
Definition

Let
\vec{p}=(m,y,a,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
and, following
[
CCI11
]
and
[
MOT23
]
, consider the Kerr metric
{\bf g}_{m,|a|}
of mass
m
and angular momentum
|a|
and rotating around the
x^{3}
-axis in Kerr-Schild coordinates
(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
given by

\displaystyle{\bf g}_{m,|a|}

\displaystyle={\bf m}+\frac{2m\tilde{r}^{3}}{\tilde{r}^{4}+|a|^{2}(x^{3})^{2}}\mathbf{k}\otimes\mathbf{k},

where
{\bf m}
is the Minkowski metric, the 1-form
\mathbf{k}
is given in coordinates by

\displaystyle\left(1,\frac{\tilde{r}x^{1}+|a|x^{2}}{\tilde{r}^{2}+|a|^{2}},\frac{\tilde{r}x^{2}-|a|x^{1}}{\tilde{r}^{2}+|a|^{2}},\frac{x^{3}}{\tilde{r}}\right),

and the function
\tilde{r}
is implicitly defined by

\displaystyle\frac{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}}{\tilde{r}^{2}+|a|^{2}}+\frac{(x^{3})^{2}}{\tilde{r}^{2}}=1.

Remark 2.16
.

The Kerr-Schild coordinates are better to read off the distance between Kerr and Minkowski than the more usual Boyer-Lindquist coordinates. Indeed, in Boyer-Lindquist coordinates, the limit of
{\bf g}_{m,|a|}
when
m
tends to 0 is

\displaystyle-\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+(r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+(r^{2}+a^{2})\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}.

This is in fact the Minkowski metric in the so-called ”oblate spheroidal” coordinates, see
[
Vis09
]
for more details.

We define a new coordinates system by setting

\displaystyle(x^{\prime})^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\,^{(v)}\Lambda^{\mu}_{\;\;\nu}\left(R^{\nu}_{\;\;\sigma}x^{\sigma}+y^{\sigma}\right).

(2.14)

where
R
is the rotation matrix such that
R\partial_{3}=\frac{a}{|a|}
(if
a=0
then
R
is the identity matrix), and
\,{}^{(v)}\Lambda
is a Lorentz boost of relative velocity
|v|
in the direction
\frac{v}{|v|}
(see (8.2) in
[
ACR23
]
).

Definition 2.17
.

We define
(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})
as the induced metric and reduced second fundamental form of the hypersurface
\{(x^{\prime})^{0}=0\}
.

For later use, we define the Lorentz factor
\gamma\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-|v|^{2}}}
.

2.4.2
Estimates for truncated black holes

Definition 2.18
.

For
\vec{p}=(m,y,a,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
, we define

\displaystyle\chi_{\vec{p}}(r)=\chi\left(\frac{r}{\lambda}\right),

with

\displaystyle\lambda\vcentcolon=\max(2|y|,2|a|,3),

where
\chi
is defined in Definition
2.4
.

The following proposition gathers important properties of the truncated initial data sets

\displaystyle\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right).

Its proof is postponed to Appendix
A.3
.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\vec{p},\vec{p}\,^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
with
m,m^{\prime}\leq 1
. Then:

(i)

The following estimates hold

\displaystyle r^{n}\left|\nabla^{n}\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)\right)\right|+r^{n+1}\left|\nabla^{n}\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma,n}\frac{m}{r},

(2.15)

and

\displaystyle r^{n}\left|\nabla^{n}\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)-\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}(g_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}-e)\right)\right|+r^{n+1}\left|\nabla^{n}\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}-\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\pi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\right)\right|

(2.16)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\lesssim_{\max(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}),n}\frac{|m-m^{\prime}|}{r}+\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(\frac{|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|}{r^{2}}+\frac{|v-v^{\prime}|}{r}\right).

(ii)

There exists functions
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},1}(\vec{p})
,
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},W_{1,0,k}}(\vec{p})
,
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},x^{k}}(\vec{p})
and
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},\Omega^{-}_{ab}}(\vec{p})
satisfying

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},1}(\vec{p})\right|+\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},W_{1,0,k}}(\vec{p})\right|+\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},x^{k}}(\vec{p})\right| +\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},\Omega^{-}_{ab}}(\vec{p})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma}m^{2},

(2.17)

and

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},1}(\vec{p})-{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},1}(\vec{p}\,^{\prime})\right|+\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},W_{1,0,k}}(\vec{p})-{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},W_{1,0,k}}(\vec{p}\,^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle+\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},x^{k}}(\vec{p})-{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},x^{k}}(\vec{p}\,^{\prime})\right|+\left|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},\Omega^{-}_{ab}}(\vec{p})-{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},\Omega^{-}_{ab}}(\vec{p}\,^{\prime})\right|

(2.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\lesssim_{\max(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})}\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(|m-m^{\prime}|+\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|+|v-v^{\prime}|\right)\right),

and such that the following integral identities hold

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{H}}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right),1\right\rangle

\displaystyle=16\pi\gamma m+{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},1}(\vec{p})

(2.19)

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{M}}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right),W_{1,0,k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-8\pi\gamma mv^{k}+{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},W_{1,0,k}}(\vec{p})

(2.20)

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{H}}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right),x^{k}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=my^{k}+{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},x^{k}}(\vec{p})

(2.21)

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{M}}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right),\Omega^{-}_{ij}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=8\pi ma^{k}+{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{M}},\Omega^{-}_{ij}}(\vec{p})

(2.22)

where in (
2.22
)
k
is such that
\in_{kij}=1
.

(iii)

We have

\displaystyle\left|\Phi\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma}\frac{m}{r^{3}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda\leq r\leq 2\lambda\}},

(2.23)

and

\displaystyle\left|\Phi\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)-\Phi\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}(g_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\pi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\right)\right|

(2.24)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim_{\max(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})}\left(\frac{|m-m^{\prime}|}{r^{3}}+\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(\frac{|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|}{r^{4}}+\frac{|v-v^{\prime}|}{r^{3}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\mathbbm{1}_{\{\min(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\leq r\leq 2\max(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\}}.

Remark 2.20
.

Thanks to the cutoff functions
\chi_{\vec{p}}
vanishing if
r\leq 3
, the
r^{-1}
weights in the estimates of Proposition
2.19
are equivalent to
(1+r)^{-1}
weights.

3
Main results

In this section, we present the main result of this article, i.e. Theorem
3.8
. Before that, we present first our conformal ansatz and then discuss the class of seed tensors we will consider in Theorem
3.8
.

3.1
A simplified conformal ansatz

Near Minkowski spacetime, we will consider the following conformal ansatz for
(g,\pi)
:

\displaystyle(g,\pi)

\displaystyle=\left(u^{4}\bar{g},\bar{\pi}+L_{e}X\right),

(3.1)

for
(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})
the parameter of the conformal formulation,
u
a scalar function and
X
a vector field. For the simplified ansatz (
3.1
), the constraint equations rewrite

\displaystyle 8\Delta_{\bar{g}}u

\displaystyle=uR(\bar{g})+\frac{1}{2}u^{-3}(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\left(\bar{\pi}+L_{e}X\right))^{2}-u^{-3}|\bar{\pi}+L_{e}X|^{2}_{\bar{g}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\bar{g}}(\bar{\pi}+L_{e}X)_{i}

\displaystyle=2u^{-1}\left(\partial_{i}u\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}(\bar{\pi}+L_{e}X)-\bar{g}^{jk}\partial_{k}u(\bar{\pi}+L_{e}X)_{ij}\right).

If
\bar{g}
is assumed to be close to the Euclidean metric and
\bar{\pi}
close to 0, we hope to show that
\widecheck{u}=u-1
and
\widecheck{X}=X
are small and we rewrite these equations as

\displaystyle 8\Delta\widecheck{u}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})+{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\bar{g},\bar{\pi},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right),

\displaystyle\Delta\widecheck{X}_{i}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{M}}(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})_{i}+{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bar{g},\bar{\pi},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)_{i},

where we defined the following remainders

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\bar{g},\bar{\pi},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)

\displaystyle=(1+\widecheck{u})^{-3}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)^{2}+\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{\pi}\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}L_{e}X-\left|L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}}-2\left(\bar{\pi}\cdot L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{\bar{g}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left((1+\widecheck{u})^{-3}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{\pi}\right)^{2}-\left|\bar{\pi}\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}}\right)+\widecheck{u}R(\bar{g})-8\left(\Delta_{\bar{g}}-\Delta\right)\widecheck{u},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\bar{g},\bar{\pi},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=2(1+\widecheck{u})^{-1}\left(\partial_{i}\widecheck{u}\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\left(\bar{\pi}+L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)-\bar{g}^{jk}\partial_{k}\widecheck{u}\left(\bar{\pi}+L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\mathrm{div}_{\bar{g}}\left(L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}-\mathrm{div}_{e}\left(L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}\right).

3.2
Parametrization by TT tensors

Here we show how, without loss of generality, we can reduce the space where the parameters
(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})
of the simplified ansatz (
3.1
) live. In the next lemma (whose proof is postponed to Appendix
B.1
), we first decompose every symmetric 2-tensor with enough decay into the sum of a TT tensor, a multiple of
e
and a Lie derivative, and give precise decay bounds.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
q=1,2
and
0<\delta<1
, and let a symmetric 2-tensor
h
in
H^{k}_{-q-\delta}
. Then there exists a unique scalar function
\mathfrak{u}[h]
and a unique vector field
\mathfrak{X}[h]
such that

\displaystyle h_{TT}\vcentcolon=h-\mathfrak{u}[h]e-\nabla\otimes\mathfrak{X}[h]

(3.2)

is a TT tensor. Moreover we have the bounds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{u}[h]\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}+\left\|\mathfrak{X}[h]\right\|_{H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}}+\left\|h_{TT}\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}.

(3.3)

Remark 3.2
.

The decomposition (
3.2
) is not orthogonal. It can be deduced from York’s classical orthogonal decomposition in
[
Yor73
]
, this one being orthogonal.

Building on Lemma
3.1
, the next proposition shows how one can benefit from the diffeomorphism invariance to simplify the parameters of the conformal ansatz. Its proof is postponed to Appendix
B.2
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
0<\delta<1
and
{\bf g}
a Lorentzian metric on
{\mathcal{R}}=\{(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\;|t|<1\}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{|t|<1}\left(\left\|{\bf g}-{\bf m}\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}(\Sigma_{t})}+\left\|\partial_{t}{\bf g}\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}(\Sigma_{t})}\right)\leq\varepsilon,

(3.4)

where
\Sigma_{t}=\{t\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\delta)>0
such that if
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, then there exists a diffeomorphism
\psi
defined on
\left\{(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\;|t|<\frac{1}{2}\right\}
, a scalar function
v
and a vector field
Y
defined on
\Sigma_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|v-1\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}(\Sigma_{0})}+\sup_{|t|<\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\psi-\mathrm{Id}\right\|_{H^{4}_{-\delta}(\Sigma_{t})}+\left\|Y\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}(\Sigma_{0})}\lesssim\varepsilon,

and such that the first and reduced second fundamental form of
\Sigma_{0}
in the spacetime

\displaystyle\left(\left\{(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\;|t|<\frac{1}{2}\right\},\psi^{*}{\bf g}\right)

are given by

\displaystyle(\psi^{*}{\bf g})_{|\Sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=v^{4}(e+\widecheck{g}),

\displaystyle\pi^{(\psi^{*}{\bf g},\Sigma_{0})}

\displaystyle=L_{e}Y+\widecheck{\pi},

with
\widecheck{g}
,
\widecheck{\pi}
TT tensors.

This proposition has the following consequence: when solving the constraint equations near Minkowski spacetime, we can assume without loss of generality that the perturbations
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
of the induced metric and of the reduced second fundamental form are TT tensors. Indeed, consider a perturbation of Minkowski with enough decay and regularity (as in (
3.4
)). From the point of view of stability problems (for instance), the choice of the initial slice is irrelevant and Proposition
3.3
tells us that the initial data on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
can be assumed to be of the form

\displaystyle\bar{g}

\displaystyle=v^{4}(e+\widecheck{g}),

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}

\displaystyle=L_{e}Y+\widecheck{\pi},

with
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
TT tensors. If we now want to solve the constraint equations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, we perturb
(\bar{g},\bar{\pi})
and look for a solution
(g,\pi)
under the form (
3.1
), i.e. look for
u
and
X
such that

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=(uv)^{4}(e+\widecheck{g}),

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=L_{e}(X+Y)+\widecheck{\pi},

solve the constraint equations. Now instead of solving for
(u,X)
, we can solve for
(uv,X+Y)
. This shows that if one’s goal is to construct every decaying solution of the constraint equations in a neighborhood of Minkowski spacetime, then there is no loss of generality in assuming that
\bar{g}-e
and
\bar{\pi}
are TT tensors.

Remark 3.4
.

Note that this parametrization by TT tensors is consistent with the number of degrees of freedom of the constraint equations. Indeed, 4 degrees of freedom come from the four equations, 4 come from the two TT tensors
\widecheck{g}
and
\widecheck{\pi}
, and 4 come from the diffeomorphism invariance, i.e. the choice of the initial slice. This adds up to 12 degrees of freedom, matching the 12 degrees of freedom of two symmetric 2-tensors.

Remark 3.5
.

In order to obtain decaying solutions to the constraint equations, we will parametrize our set of solutions by decaying TT tensors, i.e. TT tensors in
H^{2}_{-q-\delta}
for
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
0<\delta<1
. Note they can be produced by the operator
P:H^{5}_{-q-\delta+3}\longrightarrow H^{2}_{-q-\delta}
acting on symmetric 2-tensors defined by

\displaystyle P=\left(\Delta-\nabla\otimes\mathrm{div}\right)\mathrm{curl},

where the
\mathrm{curl}
of a symmetric 2-tensor is defined in Section 4.4 of
[
CK93
]
. Indeed one can show that if
h\in H^{5}_{-q-\delta+3}
then
P(h)
is TT.

3.3
Restriction to a cone

As explained in the introduction, the assumption that
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
are TT will imply a coercive estimate on the mass
m
of the black hole, i.e. for non-trivial TT tensors
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
we will have
m>0
. However, the coupling between the equation for the mass
m
and the equation for the boost velocity
v
will imply that

\displaystyle 16\pi m

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\text{lower order terms},

(3.5)

where the functional
J
is given in the following definition.

Definition 3.6
.

For
(h,\varpi)
two symmetric 2-tensors such that

\displaystyle 0<\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}<\infty,

we define the following functional

\displaystyle J(h,\varpi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1,2,3}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\varpi^{ij}\partial_{k}h_{ij}\right)^{2}}}{\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

The following proposition gathers important properties of the functional
J
. Its proof is postponed to Appendix
B.3
.

Proposition 3.7
.

The functional
J
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

If
(h,\varpi)\in\dot{H}^{1}\times L^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
, then
J(h,\varpi)<1
, where the
\dot{H}^{1}
norm is defined by
\left\|h\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}\vcentcolon=\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}}
.

(ii)

There exists a sequence
(\widecheck{g}_{n},\widecheck{\pi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of TT tensors in
\dot{H}^{1}\times L^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}J(\widecheck{g}_{n},\widecheck{\pi}_{n})=1.

The first part of Proposition
3.7
is very much reminiscent of the fact that the ADM momentum vector cannot be null unless it vanishes, see
[
AH82
]
and
[
BC96
]
. As in these references, the equality case here is discarded for decay related reasons, see Appendix
B.3
.

The second part of Proposition
3.7
shows that, even when restricted to the space of TT tensors, the functional
J
is not uniformly far from 1. This implies that the lower bound on the mass obtained from (
3.5
) cannot be uniform over the space of TT tensors and might degenerate if
J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
gets close to 1.

Therefore, we have no choice but to perform our construction over a set of TT tensors where
J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
is assumed uniformly far from 1. More precisely, we fix
0<{\alpha}\leq 1
and define the following set

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})\in\dot{H}^{1}\times L^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}~\middle|~J(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})\leq 1-{\alpha}\right\}.

Our construction will require the smallness constant
\varepsilon
measuring the size of
\widecheck{g}
and
\widecheck{\pi}
to be small compared to
{\alpha}
. This is always possible since
V_{\alpha}
has the structure of a cone and therefore by rescaling any elements of
V_{\alpha}
, one can reach any smallness with
{\alpha}
still being fixed.

3.4
Statement of the main results

We are ready to state the precise version of our main result. We recall that the cutoff
\chi_{\vec{p}}
has been introduced in Definition
2.18
.

Theorem 3.8

(Main result, version 2)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
0<\delta<1
and
0<\varepsilon\ll{\alpha}\leq 1
. Let
(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})
be two TT tensors such that

\displaystyle(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{\pi})\in V_{\alpha}\cap B_{H^{4}_{-q-\delta}\times H^{3}_{-q-\delta-1}}(0,\varepsilon),

and

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\widecheck{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}>0.

Then, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(q,\delta,{\alpha})>0
such that for every
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, the following holds.

(i)

If
q=1
, then there exists a solution
(g,\pi)
of the constraint equations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=u^{4}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}\right),

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{\pi}+L_{e}X,

where
where the black hole parameter
\vec{p}=(m,0,0,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
satisfies

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},\qquad|v|\leq 1-\frac{{\alpha}}{2},

and
u
and
X
satisfy

\displaystyle\left\|u-1\right\|_{H^{2}_{-1-\delta}}+\left\|X\right\|_{H^{2}_{-1-\delta}}\lesssim\eta\varepsilon.

(ii)

If
q=2
, then there exists a solution
(g,\pi)
of the constraint equations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=u^{4}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}\right),

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{\pi}+\breve{\pi}+L_{e}X,

where the black hole parameter
\vec{p}=(m,y,a,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
satisfies

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},\qquad|a|+|y|\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\frac{3}{2}+\delta}},\qquad|v|\leq 1-\frac{{\alpha}}{2},

and
u
,
X
and the corrector
\breve{\pi}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left\|u-1\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}}+\left\|X\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}}+\left\|\breve{\pi}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\lesssim\eta\varepsilon.

(iii)

If
q\geq 3
, then there exists a solution
(g,\pi)
of the constraint equations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=u^{4}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}+\breve{g}\right),

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{\pi}+\breve{\pi}+L_{e}X,

where the black hole parameter
\vec{p}=(m,y,a,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
satisfies

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},\qquad|a|+|y|\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{1}{q+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}},\qquad|v|\leq 1-\frac{{\alpha}}{2},

and
u
,
X
and the correctors
(\breve{g},\breve{\pi})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{q}\times{\mathcal{B}}_{q}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left\|u-1\right\|_{H^{2}_{-q-\delta}}+\left\|X\right\|_{H^{2}_{-q-\delta}}+\left\|\breve{g}\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}}+\left\|\breve{\pi}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\lesssim\eta\varepsilon.

We can adapt our construction to the time-symmetric case, which corresponds to solutions of the constraint equations with
\pi=0
. In this case, the constraint equations reduce to
R(g)=0
and there is no linear obstructions coming from the momentum constraint operator. This allows us to drop the cone assumption and to consider only
g_{\vec{p}}
with
\vec{p}=(m,y,0,0)
(which implies
\pi_{\vec{p}}=0
), i.e. all the translated Schwarzschild data sets.

Corollary 3.9

(Time-symmetric case, version 2)
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
0<\delta<1
. Let
\widecheck{g}
be a TT tensor such that
\left\|\widecheck{g}\right\|_{H^{4}_{-q-\delta}}\leq\varepsilon
and
\eta\vcentcolon=\left\|\nabla\widecheck{g}\right\|_{L^{2}}>0
. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(q,\delta)>0
such that for every
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
the following holds.

(i)

If
q=1
, then there exists a solution
g
of
R(g)=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=u^{4}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}\right),

where the black hole parameter
\vec{p}=(m,0,0,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
satisfies

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},

and
u
satisfies
\left\|u-1\right\|_{H^{2}_{-1-\delta}}\lesssim\eta\varepsilon
.

(ii)

If
q=2
, then there exists a solution
g
of
R(g)=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=u^{4}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}\right),

where the black hole parameter
\vec{p}=(m,y,0,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
satisfies

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},\qquad|y|\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\frac{3}{2}+\delta}},

and
u
satisfies
\left\|u-1\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}}\lesssim\eta\varepsilon
.

(iii)

If
q\geq 3
, then there exists a solution
g
of
R(g)=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=u^{4}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}+\breve{g}\right),

where the black hole parameter
\vec{p}=(m,y,0,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
satisfies

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\lesssim m\lesssim\eta^{2},\qquad|y|\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}\right)^{\frac{1}{q+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}},

and
u
and the corrector
\breve{g}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{q}
satisfy
\left\|u-1\right\|_{H^{2}_{-q-\delta}}+\left\|\breve{g}\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}}\lesssim\eta\varepsilon
.

Remark 3.10
.

Note that in the cases
q=1
(both in Theorem
3.8
and Corollary
3.9
), where there is no need for a nontrivial center of mass, the cutoff appearing in the ansatz for
g
or
\pi
is
\chi
defined in Definition
2.4
, i.e. we do not need
\chi_{\vec{p}}
for
q=1
.

We also give a uniqueness statement in the case of the full constraint equations with
q\geq 3
(simpler statements hold in the cases
q=1,2
). Its proof is postponed to Appendix
B.4
.

Proposition 3.11

(Uniqueness)
.

Let
q\geq 3
,
0<\delta<1
and let
(g,\pi)
be a solution of the constraint equations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Assume that there exists two sets

\displaystyle\left(\vec{p}_{i},\widecheck{g}_{i},\widecheck{\pi}_{i},\breve{g}_{i},\breve{\pi}_{i},u_{i},X_{i}\right)

(3.6)

for
i=1,2
with
\vec{p}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)
,
\widecheck{g}_{i}
and
\widecheck{\pi}_{i}
TT tensors in
H^{2}_{-q-\delta}
,
(\breve{g}_{i},\breve{\pi}_{i})\in\mathcal{A}_{q}\times\mathcal{B}_{q}
and
\left(u_{i}-1,X_{i}\right)\in(H^{2}_{-q-\delta})^{2}
such that
(g,\pi)
are given by

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=u_{i}^{4}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}_{i}}(g_{\vec{p}_{i}}-e)+\widecheck{g}_{i}+\breve{g}_{i}\right),

(3.7)

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\chi_{\vec{p}_{i}}\pi_{\vec{p}_{i}}+\widecheck{\pi}_{i}+\breve{\pi}_{i}+L_{e}X_{i},

(3.8)

Then the two sets (
3.6
) are equal.

The discussions of Sections
3.2
and
3.3
, together with the existence statement of Theorem
3.8
and the uniqueness statement of Proposition
3.11
show that we have constructed decaying solutions of the constraint equations in a neighborhood of Minkowski spacetime on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
as a graph over the set of decaying TT tensors.

4
Solving the constraint equations

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.8
in the case
q\geq 3
. The proofs of the cases
q=1,2
are simpler and we will only point out the simplifications in Remarks
4.3
,
4.9
and
4.17
. A similar remark holds for the proof of Corollary
3.9
, i.e. for the time-symmetric constraint equations, but we won’t mention the simplifications further.

4.1
Conformal formulation

We define the base metric and base reduced second fundamental form for the conformal formulation of the constraint equations.

Definition 4.1
.

We introduce the following notations:

(i)

The space of parameters is defined as

\displaystyle P={\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times B_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(0,1)\times\mathcal{A}_{q}\times\mathcal{B}_{q},

where
{\mathcal{A}}_{q}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{q}
are defined in Definition
2.14
.

(ii)

For a parameter
\mathfrak{p}=(\vec{p},\breve{g},\breve{\pi})\in P
we define the base metric
\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})
by

\displaystyle\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})

\displaystyle=e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)+\widecheck{g}+\breve{g},

and the base reduced second fundamental form
\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})
by

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})

\displaystyle=\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}+\widecheck{\pi}+\breve{\pi},

where
g_{\vec{p}}
,
\pi_{\vec{p}}
and
\chi_{\vec{p}}
are defined in Section
2.4.1
.

(iii)

We define the following distance between two parameters
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle d(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=|m-m^{\prime}|+\eta^{2}\left(|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|+|v-v^{\prime}|\right)

(4.1)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|\breve{g}-\breve{g}^{\prime}\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}}+\left\|\breve{\pi}-\breve{\pi}^{\prime}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}.

It is easy to see that
(P,d)
defines a complete metric space.

Remark 4.2
.

Note that (
2.16
) implies the rough estimate

\displaystyle r^{n}\left|\nabla^{n}\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)-\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}(g_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}-e)\right)\right|+r^{n+1}\left|\nabla^{n}\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}-\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\pi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\right)\right|

(4.2)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\lesssim_{\max(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})}\left(1+\eta^{-2}\max(m,m^{\prime})\right)\frac{d(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})}{r}.

The estimate (
4.2
) does not benefit from the fact that in (
2.16
),
|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|
is divided by
r^{2}
and not
r
. This better weight will be crucial in the proofs of Lemmas
4.15
and
4.21
.

The base metric and reduced second fundamental form
(\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p}),\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p}))
serve as parameters of the conformal method, in the sense that we look for solutions of the constraint equations under the form

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=(1+\widecheck{u})^{4}\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p}),

(4.3)

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})+L_{e}\widecheck{X},

for
\widecheck{u}
a scalar function and
\widecheck{X}
a vector field. As explained in Section
3.1
, the constraint equations for
(g,\pi)
are equivalent to the following system of equations for
\widecheck{u}
and
\widecheck{X}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} 8\Delta\widecheck{u}&={\mathcal{H}}(\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p}),\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p}))+{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right),\\
\Delta\widecheck{X}_{i}&=-{\mathcal{M}}(\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p}),\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p}))_{i}+{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)_{i},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.4)

with remainders

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)

(4.5)

\displaystyle=(1+\widecheck{u})^{-3}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)^{2}+\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}L_{e}X-\left|L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}-2\left(\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})\cdot L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left((1+\widecheck{u})^{-3}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})\right)^{2}-\left|\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}\right)+\widecheck{u}R(\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p}))-8\left(\Delta_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}-\Delta\right)\widecheck{u},

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle=2(1+\widecheck{u})^{-1}\left(\partial_{i}\widecheck{u}\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}\left(\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})+L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)-\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})^{jk}\partial_{k}\widecheck{u}\left(\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p})+L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{ij}\right)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\mathrm{div}_{\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p})}\left(L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}-\mathrm{div}_{e}\left(L_{e}\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}\right).

The strategy to prove Theorem
3.8
is then to solve (
4.4
) with a fixed point argument while also choosing the parameter
\mathfrak{p}
so that the RHS of the equations are orthogonal to the first
q^{2}
and
3q^{2}
spherical harmonics respectively appearing in the first and second equation when inverting the Laplacian operator (see Proposition
2.7
). For the sake of clarity, we define

\displaystyle A\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon={\mathcal{H}}(\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p}),\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p}))+{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right),

\displaystyle B\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=-{\mathcal{M}}(\bar{g}(\mathfrak{p}),\bar{\pi}(\mathfrak{p}))_{i}+{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\mathfrak{p},\widecheck{u},\widecheck{X}\right)_{i}.

The choice of the parameter
\mathfrak{p}
