\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\mathrm{Err}{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda\leq r\}}-\mathrm{Err}{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]\left(\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}(g_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\pi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda^{\prime}\leq r\}},x^{k}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\lesssim\max(m,m^{\prime})^{2}\left(|m-m^{\prime}|+\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|+|v-v^{\prime}|\right)\right).

It remains to study the quadratic term
Q^{k}(\vec{p})
. A direct estimate with (
2.15
) and (
2.11
) would imply

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2}D^{2}{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]\left(\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right),\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{m^{2}}{r^{4}}

so that the integral defining
Q^{k}(\vec{p})

a priori
does not converge (recall that
x^{k}\sim r
at infinity). More precisely, we introduce

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{k}_{r}(\vec{p})\vcentcolon=\int_{S_{r}}\frac{1}{2}D^{2}{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]\left(\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right),\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)\right)x^{k}

so that
Q^{k}(\vec{p})=\int_{r\geq\lambda}\widehat{Q}^{k}_{r}(\vec{p})\mathrm{d}r
. Using the expression of
(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})
we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{k}_{r}(\vec{p})=\frac{c^{k}(\vec{p})}{r}+O\left(m^{2}r^{-2}\right),

for a constant
c^{k}(\vec{p})
depending only on the parameter
\vec{p}
. If
c^{k}(\vec{p})\neq 0
, then
Q^{k}(\vec{p})=\pm\infty
which contradicts the fact that all the other terms in (
A.14
) are finite. We have thus obtained
c^{k}(\vec{p})=0
and benefiting from this cancellation we can easily show that

\displaystyle\left|Q^{k}(\vec{p})\right|\lesssim m^{2}

and

\displaystyle\left|Q^{k}(\vec{p})-Q^{k}(\vec{p}\,^{\prime})\right|\lesssim\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(|m-m^{\prime}|+\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|+|v-v^{\prime}|\right)\right).

Defining

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{H}},x^{k}}(\vec{p})\vcentcolon=Q^{k}(\vec{p})+\left\langle\mathrm{Err}{\mathcal{H}}[e,0]\left(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda\leq r\}},x^{k}\right\rangle,

and collecting the above estimates, this concludes the proof of (
2.21
). The proof of (
2.22
) is identical.

Pointwise estimates on the constraint operators.

Using the fact that
\Phi(e,0)=0
and
\Phi(g_{\vec{p}},\pi_{\vec{p}})=0
away from the center of mass
y
and the schematic expression from Lemma
2.10
we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\chi_{\vec{p}}g_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)\nabla^{2}\chi_{\vec{p}}+\chi_{\vec{p}}g_{\vec{p}}\nabla\chi_{\vec{p}}\nabla g_{\vec{p}}+(1-\chi_{\vec{p}})e\nabla^{2}(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e))

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\left((1-\chi_{\vec{p}})e\nabla(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e))\right)^{2}+(1-\chi_{\vec{p}})e\chi_{\vec{p}}g_{\vec{p}}\left(\nabla(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e))\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\chi_{\vec{p}}^{2}g_{\vec{p}}^{2}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)^{2}\left(\nabla\chi_{\vec{p}}\right)^{2}+\chi_{\vec{p}}^{3}g_{\vec{p}}^{2}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)\nabla\chi_{\vec{p}}\nabla g_{\vec{p}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+(1-\chi_{\vec{p}})e\nabla(\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}})+\chi_{\vec{p}}g_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\nabla\chi_{\vec{p}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+((1-\chi_{\vec{p}})e)^{2}\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\nabla(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e))+(1-\chi_{\vec{p}})e\chi_{\vec{p}}^{2}g_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\nabla(\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e))

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\chi_{\vec{p}}^{3}g_{\vec{p}}^{2}\pi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)\nabla\chi_{\vec{p}}+\chi_{\vec{p}}^{2}(\chi_{\vec{p}}^{2}-1)\left(\left(g_{\vec{p}}\nabla g_{\vec{p}}\right)^{2}+g_{\vec{p}}^{2}\pi_{\vec{p}}\nabla g_{\vec{p}}\right).

Using (
A.5
), (
A.8
) and (
2.15
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\Phi\left((1-\chi_{\vec{p}})e+\chi_{\vec{p}}g_{\vec{p}},\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma}\left(\frac{m}{r^{3}}+\frac{m^{2}}{r^{4}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda\leq r\leq 2\lambda\}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma}\frac{m}{r^{3}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\lambda\leq r\leq 2\lambda\}}.

Using also (
A.6
) (
A.9
) and (
2.16
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\Phi\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}}\pi_{\vec{p}}\right)-\Phi\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}(g_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}-e),\chi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\pi_{\vec{p}\,^{\prime}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim_{\max(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})}\max(m,m^{\prime})\frac{|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|}{r^{4}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\min(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\leq r\leq 2\max(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\left(\frac{|m-m^{\prime}|}{r}+\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(\frac{|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|}{r^{2}}+\frac{|v-v^{\prime}|}{r}\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}+\frac{m}{r^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\quad\times\mathbbm{1}_{\{\min(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\leq r\leq 2\max(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim_{\max(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})}\left(\frac{|m-m^{\prime}|}{r^{3}}+\max(m,m^{\prime})\left(\frac{|y-y^{\prime}|+|a-a^{\prime}|}{r^{4}}+\frac{|v-v^{\prime}|}{r^{3}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\mathbbm{1}_{\{\min(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\leq r\leq 2\max(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\}}

where we used
\left|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\right|\lesssim\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|+\left|a-a^{\prime}\right|
. This proves (
2.23
) and (
2.24
) and concludes the proof of Proposition
2.19
.

Appendix B
Appendix to Section
3

B.1
Proof of Lemma
3.1

Assuming the decomposition (
3.2
), i.e. that

\displaystyle h=\mathfrak{u}[h]e+\nabla\otimes\mathfrak{X}[h]+h_{TT}

with
h_{TT}
a TT tensor, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}h

\displaystyle=3\mathfrak{u}[h]+2\mathrm{div}\mathfrak{X}[h],

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}h

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathfrak{u}[h]+\nabla\mathrm{div}\mathfrak{X}[h]+\Delta\mathfrak{X}[h].

We apply the Laplacian to the first identity and compute the divergence of the second one:

\displaystyle\Delta\mathrm{tr}h

\displaystyle=3\Delta\mathfrak{u}[h]+2\Delta\mathrm{div}\mathfrak{X}[h],

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h

\displaystyle=\Delta\mathfrak{u}[h]+2\Delta\mathrm{div}\mathfrak{X}[h].

Provided that we can define
\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h
, this rewrites as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}[h]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{tr}h-\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\mathfrak{X}[h]

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}h.

Plugging these two relations into the second equation in (
B.1
) finally gives the system

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}[h]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{tr}h-\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h\right),

(B.2)

\displaystyle\Delta\mathfrak{X}[h]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}h-\frac{1}{4}\nabla\mathrm{tr}h-\frac{1}{4}\nabla\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h.

Conversely, one can show that if
\mathfrak{u}[h]
and
\mathfrak{X}[h]
solve (
B.2
) then
h_{TT}
defined by (
3.2
) is TT. Therefore, it only remains to show the existence of a unique solution
\left(\mathfrak{u}[h],\mathfrak{X}[h]\right)
to (
B.2
) with the appropriate bounds.

First, we need to make precise the meaning of
\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h
. Since
h\in H^{k}_{-q-\delta}
we have in particular
\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h\in H^{k-2}_{-q-\delta-2}
and thanks to the first part of Proposition
2.7
there exists a unique
\tilde{f}\in H^{k}_{-q-\delta}
with
\left\|\tilde{f}\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
such that
f
defined by

\displaystyle f=\chi(r)\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=-(j-1)}^{j-1}\langle\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h,w_{j,\ell}\rangle v_{j,\ell}+\tilde{f}

solves
\Delta f=\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h
. We then define
\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h\vcentcolon=f
. Since
\langle\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h,w_{j,\ell}\rangle=0
for
j=1,2
we have
\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h=\tilde{f}
and thus
\left\|\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
. This also shows how one solve the first equation of (
B.2
), i.e. by simply defining

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}[h]\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{tr}h-\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h\right),

which implies the bound
\left\|\mathfrak{u}[h]\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
.

We now look at the second equation of (
B.2
). Its RHS

\displaystyle Y=\mathrm{div}h-\frac{1}{4}\nabla\mathrm{tr}h-\frac{1}{4}\nabla\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}h

satisfies
\left\|Y\right\|_{H^{k-1}_{-q-\delta-1}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
. We have the following dichotomy:

•

if
q=1
, then we can use the isomorphism property of
\Delta
(see Remark
2.6
) to deduce the existence of a unique
\mathfrak{X}[h]\in H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}
solving
\Delta\mathfrak{X}[h]=Y
with moreover
\left\|\mathfrak{X}[h]\right\|_{H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
,

•

if
q=2
, then the second part of Proposition
2.7
implies the existence of a unique
\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}[h]\in H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}
with
\left\|\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}[h]\right\|_{H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
and such that
\mathfrak{X}[h]
defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}[h]

\displaystyle=\chi(r)\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=-(j-1)}^{j-1}\sum_{k=1,2,3}\langle Y,W_{j,\ell,k}\rangle V_{j,\ell,k}+\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}[h]

solves
\Delta\mathfrak{X}[h]=Y
. Since
\langle Y,W_{1,0,k}\rangle=0
,
\left|\langle Y,W_{2,\ell,k}\rangle\right|\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
and
\chi(r)r^{-2}\in H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}
, we obtain
\left\|\mathfrak{X}[h]\right\|_{H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
.

In both cases we have constructed a solution
\mathfrak{X}[h]
to the second equation of (
B.2
) with the bound
\left\|\mathfrak{X}[h]\right\|_{H^{k+1}_{-q-\delta+1}}\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k}_{-q-\delta}}
. The bound on
h_{TT}
in (
3.3
) then simply follows from its definition and the bounds on
h
,
\mathfrak{u}[h]
and
\mathfrak{X}[h]
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

B.2
Proof of Proposition
3.3

We set
\widecheck{v}=v-1
and
\widecheck{\psi}=\psi-\mathrm{Id}
and define the following symmetric 2-tensors:

\displaystyle\widecheck{g}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=(1+\widecheck{v})^{-4}\left(\left(\mathrm{Id}+\widecheck{\psi}\right)^{*}{\bf g}_{|_{\Sigma_{0}}}\right)-e,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\pi}\left(Y,\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\pi^{\left(\left(\mathrm{Id}+\widecheck{\psi}\right)^{*}{\bf g},\Sigma_{0}\right)}-L_{e}Y.

We start by expanding
\widecheck{g}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)
, already assuming
|\widecheck{v}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\widecheck{g}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\widecheck{g}(0,0)-4\widecheck{v}e+\nabla\otimes\widecheck{\psi}+\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right),

(B.3)

where the error term is given schematically by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=({\bf g}-{\bf m})\nabla\widecheck{\psi}+\left(\nabla\widecheck{\psi}\right)^{2}+\widecheck{v}\left({\bf g}-{\bf m}+\nabla\widecheck{\psi}_{i}\right)+\widecheck{v}^{2},

where
\psi_{i}
denotes spatial components of
\psi
. Note that in the previous expression of
\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)
we neglected all cubic and higher error terms since they behave better than the quadratic ones. This convention also applies to all schematic expressions in the rest of this section.

We now expand
\widecheck{\pi}\left(Y,\widecheck{\psi}\right)
. We recall the general expression

\displaystyle\pi^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}{\bf T}^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})}({\bf g}_{ij})+\frac{1}{2}{\bf g}\left(\left[{\bf T}^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})},\partial_{(i}\right],\partial_{j)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}g^{ab}{\bf T}^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})}({\bf g}_{ab})g_{ij}-g^{ab}{\bf g}\left(\left[{\bf T}^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})},\partial_{a}\right],\partial_{b}\right)g_{ij},

where
g={\bf g}_{|_{\Sigma_{0}}}
and
{\bf T}^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})}
is the future directed unit normal to
\Sigma_{0}
for
{\bf g}
. Using the schematic expansion of the unit normal for a spacetime metric close to Minkowski spacetime

\displaystyle{\bf T}^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})}-\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}({\bf g}-{\bf m})_{00}\partial_{t}+\delta^{ij}({\bf g}-{\bf m})_{0i}\partial_{j}

\displaystyle=({\bf g}-{\bf m})^{2}

and the definition of the pullback we first obtain the schematic expansion

\displaystyle{\bf T}^{\left(\left(\mathrm{Id}+\widecheck{\psi}\right)^{*}{\bf g},\Sigma_{0}\right)}-{\bf T}^{({\bf g},\Sigma_{0})}-\partial_{t}\widecheck{\psi}_{0}\partial_{t}+\delta^{ij}\left(\partial_{t}\widecheck{\psi}_{i}+\partial_{i}\widecheck{\psi}_{0}\right)\partial_{j}

\displaystyle=({\bf g}-{\bf m})\nabla\widecheck{\psi}+\left(\nabla\widecheck{\psi}\right)^{2},

and finally

\displaystyle\widecheck{\pi}\left(Y,\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\widecheck{\pi}(0,0)+\mathrm{Hess}\widecheck{\psi}_{0}-\Delta\widecheck{\psi}_{0}e-L_{e}Y+\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right),

(B.4)

where the error term is given schematically by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=({\bf g}-{\bf m})\nabla\widecheck{\psi}+\left(\nabla\widecheck{\psi}\right)^{2}+\partial_{t}({\bf g}-{\bf m})\nabla\widecheck{\psi}

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla({\bf g}-{\bf m})\nabla\widecheck{\psi}+({\bf g}-{\bf m})\nabla^{2}\widecheck{\psi}+\nabla\widecheck{\psi}\nabla^{2}\widecheck{\psi}.

Note that in this error term, time derivatives of
\widecheck{\psi}

a priori
appear but we impose
\partial_{t}\widecheck{\psi}=0
(note however that in the main terms in (
B.4
), time derivatives of
\widecheck{\psi}
actually cancel out).

We want to find
\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi},Y\right)
such that
\widecheck{g}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)
and
\widecheck{\pi}\left(Y,\widecheck{\psi}\right)
are TT tensors. According to the expansions (
B.3
) and (
B.4
) and using the notation of Lemma
3.1
this is equivalent to the following system for
\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi},Y\right)
:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{u}\left[\widecheck{g}(0,0)\right]+\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{u}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right],

(B.5)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\psi}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{X}\left[\widecheck{g}(0,0)\right]-\mathfrak{X}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right]_{i},

(B.6)

\displaystyle\Delta\widecheck{\psi}_{0}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{u}\left[\widecheck{\pi}(0,0)\right]+2\mathfrak{u}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right]+2\mathrm{div}\mathfrak{X}\left[\widecheck{\pi}(0,0)\right]+2\mathrm{div}\mathfrak{X}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right],

(B.7)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\widecheck{\psi}_{0}+\mathfrak{X}\left[\widecheck{\pi}(0,0)\right]+\mathfrak{X}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right],

(B.8)

where we used
\mathfrak{u}[h_{1}+h_{2}]=\mathfrak{u}[h_{1}]+\mathfrak{u}[h_{2}]
and
\mathfrak{X}[h_{1}+h_{2}]=\mathfrak{X}[h_{1}]+\mathfrak{X}[h_{2}]
which follows from the uniqueness part of Lemma
3.1
. Indeed, one can check that if
\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi},Y\right)
satisfy (
B.5
)-(
B.8
) then we have

\displaystyle\widecheck{g}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\widecheck{g}(0,0)+\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right)_{TT},

\displaystyle\widecheck{\pi}\left(Y,\widecheck{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\widecheck{\pi}(0,0)+\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right)_{TT}.

It thus remains to solve the system (
B.5
)-(
B.7
) on
\Sigma_{0}
via a Banach fixed point argument and then define
Y
by (
B.8
). We don’t write down all the details and simply state
a priori
estimates which easily imply both the boundedness and contraction part of the fixed point argument.

First, note that the assumption (
3.4
) together with the bounds of Lemma
3.1
in the cases
(k,q)=(3,1)
and
(k,q)=(2,2)
implies

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{u}[\widecheck{g}(0,0)]\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}+\left\|\mathfrak{X}[\widecheck{g}(0,0)]\right\|_{H^{4}_{-\delta}}+\left\|\mathfrak{u}[\widecheck{\pi}(0,0)]\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}}+\left\|\mathfrak{X}[\widecheck{\pi}(0,0)]\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

(B.9)

We assume that there exists
A_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\widecheck{v}\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}+\left\|\widecheck{\psi}\right\|_{H^{4}_{-\delta}}\leq A_{0}\varepsilon.

Using also (
B.9
), this implies the following estimates on the error terms

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}+\left\|\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}}\lesssim C(A_{0})\varepsilon^{2},

where we also used the embeddings
H^{3}_{-1-\delta}\times H^{3}_{-1-\delta}\subset H^{3}_{-1-\delta}
,
H^{3}_{-1-\delta}\times H^{3}_{-1-\delta}\subset H^{2}_{-2-\delta}
and
H^{3}_{-1-\delta}\times H^{2}_{-2-\delta}\subset H^{2}_{-2-\delta}
from Lemma
2.3
. Thanks to the bounds of Lemma
3.1
in the cases
(k,q)=(3,1)
and
(k,q)=(2,2)
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{u}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right]\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}+\left\|\mathfrak{X}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{g}}\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right]\right\|_{H^{4}_{-\delta}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\left\|\mathfrak{u}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right]\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}}+\left\|\mathfrak{X}\left[\mathrm{Err}_{\widecheck{\pi}}\left(\widecheck{\psi}\right)\right]\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}\lesssim C(A_{0})\varepsilon^{2}.

(B.10)

We now estimate
\left(\widecheck{v},\widecheck{\psi}\right)
with the equations (
B.5
)-(
B.7
). For
\widecheck{v}
and
\widecheck{\psi}_{i}
we directly obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\widecheck{v}\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}+\left\|\widecheck{\psi}_{i}\right\|_{H^{4}_{-\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon+C(A_{0})\varepsilon^{2},

where we used (
B.9
) and (
B.2
). For
\widecheck{\psi}_{0}
, we estimate the RHS of (
B.7
):

\displaystyle\left\|\text{RHS of \eqref{eq phi 0}}\right\|_{H^{2}_{-2-\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon+C(A_{0})\varepsilon^{2},

where we used (
B.9
) and (
B.2
). We then use the isomorphism property of
\Delta
(see Remark
2.6
) to deduce the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\widecheck{\psi}_{0}\right\|_{H^{4}_{-\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon+C(A_{0})\varepsilon^{2}.

Thanks to these
a priori
estimates we can solve the nonlinear system (
B.5
)-(
B.7
) and obtain the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\widecheck{v}\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}+\left\|\widecheck{\psi}\right\|_{H^{4}_{-\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Defining
Y
by (
B.8
), (
B.9
) and (
B.2
) imply

\displaystyle\left\|Y\right\|_{H^{3}_{-1-\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.3
.

B.3
Proof of Proposition
3.7

The first part of Proposition
3.7
is proved as follows. We define
c_{k}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\varpi^{ij}\partial_{k}h_{ij}
. We apply Cauchy-Schwarz in
L^{2}
and then in
\ell^{2}
and get
|c_{k}|^{2}\leq\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\|\partial_{k}h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
which gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{k=1,2,3}|c_{k}|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\sum_{k=1,2,3}\left\|\partial_{k}h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We now apply the inequality
ab\leq\frac{a^{2}}{2}+\frac{b^{2}}{2}
with
a=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}}
and
b=\sqrt{2}\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}
and obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{k=1,2,3}|c_{k}|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

i.e.
J(h,\varpi)\leq 1
.

If we assume
J(h,\varpi)=1
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1,2,3}\left(\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\|\partial_{k}h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-|c_{k}|^{2}\right)=0.

Since these three quantities are nonnegative, they must all vanish and for all
k=1,2,3
we have
|c_{k}|^{2}=\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\|\partial_{k}h\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Therefore, we have the equality case in the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, i.e. there exists
\lambda_{k}
for
k=1,2,3
such that
\partial_{k}h_{ij}=\lambda_{k}\varpi_{ij}
for all
i,j=1,2,3
. Moreover, the equality case in the inequality
ab\leq\frac{a^{2}}{2}+\frac{b^{2}}{2}
implies that
\left\|\nabla h\right\|_{L^{2}}=2\left\|\varpi\right\|_{L^{2}}
, which in turn implies that
\sum_{k=1,2,3}\lambda_{k}^{2}=4
. Therefore, the vector
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
is non-zero and there exists a non-zero vector
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
such that
X^{k}\lambda_{k}=0
. This implies that each
h_{ij}
satisfies the transport equation
X(h_{ij})=0
. This is incompatible with
h
being in
\dot{H}^{1}
and non-zero. Therefore the equality case is impossible and we have proved that

\displaystyle J(h,\varpi)<1.

We now turn to the second part of Proposition
3.7
. As formally shown above, up to a linear change of coordinates, the upper bound 1 is reached when
\widecheck{\pi}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}
. Therefore, in order to saturate the bound
J<1
, we will construct a sequence
(\widecheck{g}_{n},\widecheck{\pi}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of TT tensors with
\widecheck{\pi}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}
. We first note that if
(\widecheck{g}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of symmetric 2-tensors such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\left\|\partial_{1}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}}{\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\left\|\partial_{2}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}}{\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}}=0,

(B.11)

then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}J\left(\sqrt{2}\widecheck{g}_{n},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right)=1.

Indeed we have:

\displaystyle J\left(\sqrt{2}\widecheck{g}_{n},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{4}+\sum_{k=1,2}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}^{ij}\partial_{k}(\widecheck{g}_{n})_{ij}\right)^{2}}}{\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\partial_{1}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\partial_{2}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+\frac{\left\|\partial_{1}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}+\frac{\left\|\partial_{2}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}}.

Thanks to the assumption (
B.11
) this last quantity converges to 1, and thanks to the first part of Proposition
3.7
we have
J\left(\sqrt{2}\widecheck{g}_{n},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right)<1
so that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}J\left(\sqrt{2}\widecheck{g}_{n},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right)=1.

In view of this fact, in order to prove the second part of Proposition
3.7
it is enough to exhibit a sequence
(\widecheck{g}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of TT tensors satisfying (
B.11
). To this end, we first consider
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
a non-zero compactly supported function,
\chi:{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
a smooth cutoff function such that
\chi=1
on
[0,1]
and
\chi=0
on
[2,+\infty)
and define

\displaystyle g_{n}\vcentcolon=\phi^{\prime\prime}(x^{3})\chi\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)M,

where
\rho=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}}
and
M=(\mathrm{d}x^{1})^{2}-(\mathrm{d}x^{2})^{2}
. This defines a traceless symmetric 2-tensor on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}g_{n})_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\phi^{\prime\prime}(x^{3})\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\frac{x^{1}}{\rho},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}g_{n})_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n}\phi^{\prime\prime}(x^{3})\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\frac{x^{2}}{\rho},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}g_{n})_{3}

\displaystyle=0.

Moreover, the sequence
g_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{3}g_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=c_{0}n,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{div}g_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{1}g_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{2}g_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

with

\displaystyle c_{0}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{4\pi\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(z))^{2}\mathrm{d}z\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\chi(\rho)\rho\mathrm{d}\rho\right)}>0,

and where the implicit constant in
\lesssim
depends on
\phi
and
\chi
. We need to correct the fact that
g_{n}
is not divergence free. We define a symmetric 2-tensor
h_{n}
directly in Cartesian coordinates:

•

we first define the
(h_{n})_{3i}
components for
i=1,2
by
\partial_{3}(h_{n})_{3i}=-(\mathrm{div}g_{n})_{i}
with the requirement
\lim_{x^{3}\to-\infty}(h_{n})_{3i}=0
,

•

we then define the
(h_{n})_{33}
component by
\partial_{3}(h_{n})_{33}=-\partial_{1}(h_{n})_{31}-\partial_{2}(h_{n})_{32}
with the requirement
\lim_{x^{3}\to-\infty}(h_{n})_{33}=0
,

•

we finally define the remaining components
(h_{n})_{ij}
for
i,j=1,2
as the solution to

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}^{(2)}h_{n}

\displaystyle=0,

(B.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}^{(2)}h_{n}

\displaystyle=-(h_{n})_{33},

where
\mathrm{div}^{(2)}
and
\mathrm{tr}^{(2)}
denote the divergence and trace operator in the
(x^{1},x^{2})
variables.

By construction
\widecheck{g}_{n}\vcentcolon=g_{n}+h_{n}
is a TT tensor. It remains to show that the sequence
(\widecheck{g}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies (
B.11
), and before that to properly define and estimate
h_{n}
.

The components
(h_{n})_{3i}
for
i=1,2,3
.

From the definition of
(h_{n})_{31}
and
(h_{n})_{32}
and the expression of
\mathrm{div}g_{n}
we have

\displaystyle(h_{n})_{31}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n}\phi^{\prime}(x^{3})\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\frac{x^{1}}{\rho},

\displaystyle(h_{n})_{32}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\phi^{\prime}(x^{3})\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\frac{x^{2}}{\rho}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{3}(h_{n})_{33}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{1}(h_{n})_{31}-\partial_{2}(h_{n})_{32}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\phi^{\prime}(x^{3})\left(\partial_{1}\left(\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\frac{x^{1}}{\rho}\right)-\partial_{2}\left( \chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\frac{x^{2}}{\rho}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\phi^{\prime}(x^{3})\frac{(x^{1})^{2}-(x^{2})^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\chi^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)-\frac{1}{\rho}\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\right),

which in turn yields

\displaystyle(h_{n})_{33}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\phi(x^{3})\frac{(x^{1})^{2}-(x^{2})^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\chi^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)-\frac{1}{\rho}\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\rho}{n}\right)\right).

For
i,j=1,2
, we obtain the estimates

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{3}(h_{n})_{3i}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{j}(h_{n})_{3i}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{3}(h_{n})_{33}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{j}(h_{n})_{33}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

The components
(h_{n})_{ij}
for
i,j=1,2
.

We first define a scalar function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by

\displaystyle f(x^{1},x^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(x^{1})^{2}-(x^{2})^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\left(\chi^{\prime\prime}(\rho)-\frac{1}{\rho}\chi^{\prime}(\rho)\right)

so that
(h_{n})_{33}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\phi(x^{3})f\left(\frac{x^{1}}{n},\frac{x^{2}}{n}\right)
. We now search
(h_{n})_{ij}
under the form

\displaystyle(h_{n})_{ij}(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\phi(x^{3})\tilde{h}_{ij}\left(\frac{x^{1}}{n},\frac{x^{2}}{n}\right),

where
\tilde{h}
is a symmetric 2-tensor defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. We also define
\hat{h}=\tilde{h}+\frac{1}{2}fe^{(2)}
(where
e^{(2)}
is the Euclidean metric on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
). The system (
B.12
) is then equivalent to the following system for
\hat{h}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}^{(2)}\hat{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{(2)}f,

(B.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}^{(2)}\hat{h}

\displaystyle=0,

where
\nabla^{(2)}=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2})
. We now use a consequence of Corollary 2.11 from
[
Hun16
]
on the divergence equation on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
for traceless symmetric 2-tensor: since
f
is smooth and compactly supported and since
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\partial_{i}f=0
, there exists a traceless symmetric 2-tensor
\hat{h}
solution of (
B.13
) and such that

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{h}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}+\left\|\nabla\hat{h}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\lesssim 1,

where the implicit constant depends on
f
, i.e. on
\chi
and
\phi
. We thus get the estimates for
k,i,j=1,2

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{3}(h_{n})_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{k}(h_{n})_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

Verification of (
B.11
).

Thanks to
\left\|\partial_{3}g_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}=c_{0}n
and the various estimates on
\left\|\partial_{3}h_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}
above we have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}\geq c_{0}n-c_{1}-\frac{c_{2}}{n},

for some
c_{1},c_{2}>0
. Therefore there exists
n_{0}
such that if
n\geq n_{0}
then
\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{2}n
. For
n\geq n_{0}
we then have

\displaystyle\frac{\left\|\partial_{1}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{2}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}}{\left\|\partial_{3}\widecheck{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1+\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}}{n}.

This shows that the sequence of TT tensors
(\widecheck{g}_{n})_{n\geq n_{0}}
satisfies (
B.11
). This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.7
.

B.4
Proof of Proposition
3.11

From the decay assumptions on
\widecheck{g}_{i}
,
\widecheck{\pi}_{i}
,
u_{i}-1
and
X_{i}
, the support properties of
\breve{g}_{i}
and
\breve{\pi}_{i}
, and (
3.7
)-(
3.8
) we deduce that for large
r
we have

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=e+\chi_{\vec{p}_{i}}(g_{\vec{p}_{i}}-e)+O\left(r^{-q-\delta}\right),

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\chi_{\vec{p}_{i}}\pi_{\vec{p}_{i}}+O\left(r^{-q-\delta-1}\right),

for
i=1,2
. Therefore the asymptotics charges of
(g,\pi)
are given by the ones of
(g_{\vec{p}_{i}},\pi_{\vec{p}_{i}})
. Since the asymptotic charges completely determine the black hole parameter
\vec{p}
, we have
\vec{p}_{1}=\vec{p}_{2}=\vcentcolon\vec{p}
. We then compute the trace of
g
with respect to the Euclidean metric from (
3.7
). Since
\widecheck{g}_{i}
and
\breve{g}_{i}
are traceless by assumption (recall that tensors in
{\mathcal{A}}_{q}
are traceless, see Proposition
2.13
) this gives

\displaystyle u^{4}_{1}\mathrm{tr}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)\right)=u^{4}_{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(e+\chi_{\vec{p}}(g_{\vec{p}}-e)\right)

and implies
u_{1}=u_{2}
and furthermore

\displaystyle\widecheck{g}_{1}+\breve{g}_{1}=\widecheck{g}_{2}+\breve{g}_{2}.

Since
\widecheck{g}_{i}
is divergence-free we can take the divergence of this identity and get
\mathrm{div}(\breve{g}_{1}-\breve{g}_{2})=0
. Remember now that
\breve{g}_{1}-\breve{g}_{2}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{q}
is a linear combination of
h_{j,\ell}
(recall Definition
2.14
) and that the 1-forms
\mathrm{div}h_{j,\ell}
are linearly independent (see Proposition
2.13
). Therefore we obtain
\breve{g}_{1}=\breve{g}_{2}
and then
\widecheck{g}_{1}=\widecheck{g}_{2}
.

We now look at the reduced second fundamental form, (
3.8
) gives

\displaystyle\widecheck{\pi}_{1}+\breve{\pi}_{1}+L_{e}X_{1}

\displaystyle=\widecheck{\pi}_{2}+\breve{\pi}_{2}+L_{e}X_{2}.

We apply the operator
\Delta\mathrm{tr}+\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div}
to this equality and use the fact that
(\Delta\mathrm{tr}+\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div})(L_{e}X)=0
for all vector field
X
and the fact that
\widecheck{\pi}_{i}
is TT, we obtain

\displaystyle(\Delta\mathrm{tr}+\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div})(\breve{\pi}_{1}-\breve{\pi}_{2})=0.

Remember now that
\breve{\pi}_{1}-\breve{\pi}_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{q}
is a linear combination of
\varpi_{Z}
(recall Definition
2.14
) and that the functions
(\Delta\mathrm{tr}+\mathrm{div}\mathrm{div})\varpi_{Z}
are linearly independent (see Proposition
2.13
). Therefore we obtain
\breve{\pi}_{1}=\breve{\pi}_{2}
and

\displaystyle\widecheck{\pi}_{1}+L_{e}X_{1}

\displaystyle=\widecheck{\pi}_{2}+L_{e}X_{2}.

We take the divergence of this equality and get
\Delta(X_{1}-X_{2})=0
(since
\widecheck{\pi}_{i}
is divergence-free). Thanks to the decay property of
X_{i}
, this implies
X_{1}=X_{2}
and finally
\widecheck{\pi}_{1}=\widecheck{\pi}_{2}
.
